<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:shell-dim}
For $j\ge 1$, the following bounds hold with constants depending only on $d$ and $\chi$, and in (d) also on $\|g\|_\infty$.  The mass $m$ enters only through the lower bounds in (a) and (e) and, when $d=2$, through $q_0$ in the upper bound in (e).
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $q_j\asymp2^{(d-2)j}$.
\item $\|Q_j\|_{L^1(\mathbb{R}^d)}=O(2^{-2j})$.
\item $\|Q_j\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d)}=q_j$.
\item For a smooth, nonnegative, compactly supported $g$ with $K:=\operatorname{supp} g$, the row integral $A_j:=\sup_{x\in K}\int g(y)\,|Q_j(x-y)|\,dy$ satisfies $A_j=O(2^{-2j})$.
\item For $J\ge1$, the cumulative Wick constant $D_J:=\sum_{j=0}^{J}q_j$ satisfies $D_J\asymp J$ if $d=2$ and $D_J\asymp2^{(d-2)J}$ if $d\ge3$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
\textit{Part (a).} We first prove that $q_j$ is bounded above. Since $S_j$ is supported on the annulus $2^{j-1}\le|p|\le 2^{j+1}$ and satisfies $S_j(p)\le 4\cdot 2^{-2j}$, and the volume of this annulus in $\mathbb{R}^d$ is $O(2^{dj})$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q_j-upper}
    q_j
    \;\le\; \frac{4\cdot 2^{-2j}\cdot O(2^{dj})}{(2\pi)^d}
    \;=\; C\,2^{(d-2)j}\;.
\end{equation}
For the lower bound, substitute $u=2^{-j}p$ into \eqref{eq:qj-def}:
\begin{equation*}
    q_j \;=\; \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\frac{2^{dj}\,d^du}{(2\pi)^d}\,
    \frac{\chi(u)}{2^{2j}u^2+m^2}\,.
\end{equation*}
On the support of $\chi$, we have $u^2\le 4$, so $2^{2j}u^2+m^2\le(4+m^2)\,2^{2j}$. Therefore,
\begin{equation}
    q_j \;\ge\;
    \frac{2^{(d-2)j}}{(4+m^2)\,(2\pi)^d}
    \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\chi(u)\,d^du
    \;=\; c\,2^{(d-2)j}\;,
\end{equation}
with $c>0$ since $\chi\ge 0$ and $\chi\not\equiv 0$.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Part (b).}
Substituting $u=2^{-j}p$ in~\eqref{eq:Qj-kernel} gives
\begin{equation*}
    Q_j(z) \;=\; 2^{(d-2)j}\,\hat{G}_j\big(2^jz\big)\;,
\end{equation*}
where
\begin{equation*}
    G_j(u) \;:=\; \frac{\chi(u)}{u^2+m^2\,2^{-2j}}\;,
    \qquad
    \hat{G}_j(w) \;:=\; \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\frac{d^du}{(2\pi)^d}\,G_j(u)\,\cos(u\cdot w)\;.
\end{equation*}
Changing variables in the $z$ integral gives $\|Q_j\|_{L^1}=2^{(d-2)j}\cdot2^{-dj}\|\hat{G}_j\|_{L^1}=2^{-2j}\|\hat{G}_j\|_{L^1}$.  It therefore suffices to bound $\|\hat{G}_j\|_{L^1}$ uniformly in $j$.

Two properties of $G_j$ give this uniform bound.  First, it is supported in the fixed annulus $\{\tfrac12\le|u|\le2\}$, where $1/4\leq u^2\leq4$ bounds for every $j$ both the denominators and the powers of $u$ that result from differentiation. Thus, $G_j$ and its derivatives are bounded, along with their $L^1$ norms, by constants free of $j$ and~$m$.  Second, $\hat{G}_j$ decays faster than any power of $|w|$.  Fix $N$ with $2N>d$.  Since $\Delta_u\cos(u\cdot w)=-|w|^2\cos(u\cdot w)$, each Laplacian moved off the cosine gives a factor $|w|^{-2}$, and because $G_j$ is smooth and compactly supported, moving $N$ of them onto $G_j$ leaves no boundary terms:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ghat-decay}
    \big|\hat{G}_j(w)\big|
    \;=\; \frac{1}{|w|^{2N}}
    \bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\frac{d^du}{(2\pi)^d}\,
    \big(\Delta^NG_j\big)(u)\,\cos(u\cdot w)\bigg|
    \;\le\; \frac{\big\|\Delta^NG_j\big\|_{L^1}}
    {(2\pi)^d\,|w|^{2N}}\;.
\end{equation}
Bounding $|\hat{G}_j|\le(2\pi)^{-d}\|G_j\|_{L^1}$ on $|w|\le1$ and using~\eqref{eq:Ghat-decay} on $|w|>1$, where $\int_{|w|>1}|w|^{-2N}d^dw<\infty$ because $2N>d$, gives $\|\hat{G}_j\|_{L^1}\le C$ with $C$ free of $j$.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Part (c).}
Since $S_j\ge0$ and $|\cos|\le 1$ in \eqref{eq:Qj-kernel}, we have $|Q_j(z)|\le Q_j(0)=q_j$ for all $z$, with equality at $z=0$.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Part (d).}
By (b), $A_j\le\|g\|_\infty\,\|Q_j\|_{L^1}=O(2^{-2j})$.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Part (e).}
Part (a) bounds $q_j$ for $j\ge1$, and it remains to bound $q_0$. The zeroth shell has $0\le\chi_0\le1$ and is supported in $\{|p|\le2\}$, so
\begin{equation*}
    q_0 \;\le\; \int_{|p|\le2}\frac{d^dp}{(2\pi)^d}\,\frac{1}{p^2+m^2}\;,
\end{equation*}
which equals $\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(1+4/m^2)$ at $d=2$, and, because $|p|^{-2}$ is integrable at the origin when $d\ge3$, is at most a constant depending only on $d$ otherwise.

At $d=2$ each $q_j$ with $j\ge1$ lies between two constants $c$ and $C$, so for $J\ge1$
\begin{equation*}
    cJ\;\le\;D_J\;\le\;q_0+CJ\;\le\;(q_0+C)\,J\;.
\end{equation*}
At $d\ge3$ the bounds $q_j\le C\,2^{(d-2)j}$ form a geometric series with ratio at least $2$, whose sum up to $J$ is at most twice its last term, so
\begin{equation*}
    c\,2^{(d-2)J}\;\le\;q_J\;\le\;D_J\;\le\;q_0+2C\,2^{(d-2)J}\;\le\;(q_0+2C)\,2^{(d-2)J}\;.
\end{equation*}
In both cases, $D_J$ lies between constant multiples of the claimed rate, which is (e).
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:coincident}
The cumulative Wick constant $D_\Lambda=\sum_{j=0}^\Lambda q_j$ controls the coincident-point singularity of the free field truncated at shell $\Lambda$.  For a fixed $\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}$, related to the frequency cutoff by $\Lambda=\log_2(\Lambda_{\mathrm{UV}}/m)$ up to an additive constant,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:D_scaling}
    D_\Lambda \;\asymp\;
    \begin{cases}
    \log(\Lambda_{\mathrm{UV}}/m) & d=2
    \quad\text{(logarithmic)}\;,\\
    \Lambda_{\mathrm{UV}} & d=3
    \quad\text{(linear)}\;,\\
    \Lambda_{\mathrm{UV}}^2 & d=4
    \quad\text{(quadratic)}\;,
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}
all of which are the standard scaling of the Wick constant with the upper frequency cutoff $\Lambda_{\mathrm{UV}}$. Wick ordering removes this divergence from the interaction.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:shell-field}
Define the $\ell$-th \emph{neuron} of shell $j$ at spatial point $x$ (conditioned on features $\omega_j$) by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:feature-vec}
    U_{j,\ell}(x)
    \;:=\; \sqrt{\frac{2q_j}{N_j\sigma^2}}\;
    \cos(b_{j,\ell}\cdot x + c_{j,\ell})\;,
\end{equation}
and write $U_j(x):=(U_{j,1}(x),\ldots, U_{j,N_j}(x))^T\in\mathbb{R}^{N_j}$ for the vector of the shell's neurons at~$x$.  The $j$-th \emph{shell field} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shell-field}
    \varphi_j(x)
    \;:=\; \sum_{\ell=1}^{N_j}
    a_{j,\ell}\,U_{j,\ell}(x)\;,
\end{equation}
where $a_j=(a_{j,1},\ldots,a_{j,N_j})^T \sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2 I_{N_j})$ is a vector of Gaussian coefficients (``weights'') independent of the features and of all other shells.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:quenched-cov}
Conditioned on features $\omega_j$, the shell field $\varphi_j(x)$ is a centered Gaussian process in $a_j$ with covariance
\begin{equation}\label{eq:quenched-cov}
    \Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)
    \;:=\; \sigma^2\,U_j(x)^T\,U_j(y)
    \;=\; \frac{2q_j}{N_j}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_j}
    \cos(b_{j,\ell}\cdot x + c_{j,\ell})\,
    \cos(b_{j,\ell}\cdot y + c_{j,\ell})\;.
\end{equation}
Its diagonal value, the \emph{quenched Wick constant}, is $q_j^\omega(x):=\Gamma_j^\omega(x,x)$. Here and below, \emph{quenched} is used as in the theory of disordered systems: the features are frozen disorder, drawn once and held fixed, while the coefficients $a_j$ remain the degrees of freedom being integrated.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:cov-mean}
The expectation of the quenched covariance over the features recovers the deterministic shell kernel:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cov-mean}
    \mathbb{E}_{\omega_j}\!\big[\Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)\big]
    \;=\; Q_j(x-y)\;.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Each summand of~\eqref{eq:quenched-cov} is an i.i.d.\ copy of $2q_j\cos(b_j\cdot x+c_j)\cos(b_j\cdot y+c_j)$.  By the product-to-sum formula for cosines and the independence of $b$ and $c$:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}\big[2q_j\cos(b\cdot x+c)\cos(b\cdot y+c)\big]
    &\;=\; q_j\,\mathbb{E}_b\!\big[\cos(b\cdot(x-y))\big]
    + q_j\,\mathbb{E}_b\!\big[\mathbb{E}_c[\cos(b\cdot(x+y)+2c)]\big]
    \notag\\
    &\;=\; q_j\,\mathbb{E}_b\!\big[\cos(b\cdot(x-y))\big]\;,
    \label{eq:cos-mean}
\end{align}
since $\mathbb{E}_c[\cos(\theta+2c)]=0$ for any $\theta$.  The remaining expectation over $b\sim\pi_j$ is
\begin{equation}
    q_j\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\pi_j(b)\,\cos(b\cdot(x-y))\,d^db
    \;=\; \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\frac{d^db}{(2\pi)^d}\,S_j(b)\,
    \cos(b\cdot(x-y))
    \;=\; Q_j(x-y)\;,
\end{equation}
by the definition \eqref{eq:features} of $\pi_j$ and the definition \eqref{eq:Qj-kernel} of $Q_j$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:incr-var}
For every $J\ge0$ and all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d$, conditioned on the features,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:incr-var}
    \operatorname{Var}\big(B_J(x)-B_J(y)\mid\omega\big)
    \;\le\; \Upsilon_J\,|x-y|^2 ,
    \qquad
    \Upsilon_J \;:=\; \tfrac{32}{3}\Big(\max_{j\le J}q_j\Big)4^{J}\;,
\end{equation}
and $\Upsilon_J$ does not depend on the widths $(N_j)_{j\leq J}$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Conditioned on features, $B_J(x)-B_J(y)$ is a linear combination of the weights with fixed coefficients, and the weights are independent and centered with variance $\sigma^2$:
\begin{equation*}
    \operatorname{Var}\big(B_J(x)-B_J(y)\mid\omega\big)
    \;=\;\sigma^2\sum_{j\le J}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_j}
    \big(U_{j,\ell}(x)-U_{j,\ell}(y)\big)^2 .
\end{equation*}
Since $\cos$ is $1$-Lipschitz, and $|b_{j,\ell}|\leq2^{j+1}$ on the support of $\pi_j$,
\begin{equation*}
   |U_{j,\ell}(x)-U_{j,\ell}(y)|\le\sqrt{2q_j/(N_j\sigma^2)}\,2^{j+1}\,|x-y|\;. 
\end{equation*}
Squaring and summing over the $N_j$ features of shell $j$ cancels the width,
\begin{equation*}
    \sigma^2\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_j}\big(U_{j,\ell}(x)-U_{j,\ell}(y)\big)^2
    \;\le\;2q_j4^{j+1}|x-y|^2\;,
\end{equation*}
and summing over $j\le J$ gives $\sum_{j\le J}2q_j4^{j+1}\le8\big(\max_{j\le J}q_j\big)\tfrac{4^{J+1}-1}{3}\le\Upsilon_J$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:no-counterterms}
Wick ordering the interaction removes the coincident-point divergence, and in $d=2$ no further counterterms are needed because the shell contributions to $\log Z_\Lambda$ are $O(2^{-2j}j^3)$ (\Cref{thm:d2-convergence}).  Above two dimensions, Wick ordering alone no longer suffices.  In $d=3$ a mass counterterm is needed because the propagator diverges like $|x-y|^{-1}$ rather than logarithmically, so products of three or more propagators between distinct points are no longer integrable. Developing that structure is outside the scope of this paper (\Cref{sec:higher-dim}).
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:V-lower}
The Wick-ordered quartic obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:H4-lower}
    H_4(u;D)\;\ge\;-6D^2\qquad\text{for all }u\in\mathbb{R},\ D\ge0\;.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for all $0\leq J\leq\Lambda$ and all coefficients $a_0,\dots,a_J$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:V-det-lower}
    V_J \;\ge\;
    -6t\int g\,\big(D^\omega_J\big)^2dx\;,
\end{equation}
and in particular $e^{-V_J}\le e^{C_V(J)}$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CV-def}
    C_V(J) \;:=\; 6t\|g\|_1\big\|D^\omega_J\big\|_\infty^2
    \;<\;\infty \;.
\end{equation}
Moreover $1\le Z_J\le e^{C_V(J)}$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Completing the square, $H_4(u;D)=(u^2-3D)^2-6D^2\ge-6D^2$, which is~\eqref{eq:H4-lower}; substituted into~\eqref{eq:V-def}, it gives~\eqref{eq:V-det-lower}.  Bounding that integral by $\|g\|_1\|D^\omega_J\|_\infty^2$ makes $-V_J\le C_V(J)$ uniformly in the coefficients.  The constant $C_V(J)$ is finite because $\cos^2\le1$ in~\eqref{eq:quenched-cov} gives $q^\omega_j\le2q_j$ pointwise, and hence $\|D^\omega_J\|_\infty\le2D_J$.  Thus $e^{-V_J}\le e^{C_V(J)}$, and $Z_J\le e^{C_V(J)}$ follows.  For the lower bound, Jensen's inequality for the convex function $u\mapsto e^{-u}$ gives $Z_J\ge e^{-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma_J}[V_J]}=1$, since the field is distributed as $B_J(x)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,D^\omega_J(x))$ under $\gamma_J$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:positivity}
Conditionally on $\mathcal{F}_{j-1}$ and $\omega_j$, the shell interaction has mean zero, $\mathbb{E}_{a_j}[X_j\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1},\omega_j]=0$, and consequently $\Psi_j\ge0$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Conditionally on $\mathcal{F}_{j-1}$ and $\omega_j$, the background is fixed, and the shell field $\varphi_j(x)$ is a centered Gaussian of variance $q_j^\omega(x)$ (\Cref{def:quenched-cov}).  That variance is exactly the value at which $X_j$ evaluates the Wick constant of $T$, so \eqref{eq:shell-mean-zero} applies pointwise in $x$ with $z=\varphi_j(x)$ and $q=q_j^\omega(x)$:
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}_{a_j}\big[T\big(B_{j-1}(x),\,D^\omega_{j-1}(x),\,q_j^\omega(x);\,\varphi_j(x)\big)
    \mid \mathcal{F}_{j-1},\omega_j\big] = 0
    \qquad\text{for each }x.
\end{equation}
Integrating against $g(x)\,dx$ gives $\mathbb{E}_{a_j}[X_j\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1},\omega_j]=0$.  Jensen's inequality for the convex function $u\mapsto e^{-u}$ then gives $e^{\Psi_j}=\mathbb{E}_{a_j}[e^{-X_j}]\ge e^{-\mathbb{E}_{a_j}[X_j]}=1$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:telescope}
For $1\le j\le\Lambda$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:telescope-step}
    Z_j \;=\; Z_{j-1}\,\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\big]\;,
\end{equation}
and consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:telescope}
    Z_\Lambda \;=\; Z_0\,\prod_{j=1}^{\Lambda}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\big]\;.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Write $V_j = V_{j-1}+X_j$ and $\gamma_j = \gamma_{j-1}\otimes\gamma_\sigma(a_j)$.  Since $V_{j-1}$ is $\mathcal{F}_{j-1}$-measurable and hence independent of $a_j$ under the product prior, Fubini gives
\begin{equation*}
    Z_j \;=\; \int e^{-V_{j-1}-X_j}\,d\gamma_j
    \;=\; \int e^{-V_{j-1}}\,
    \bigg(\int e^{-X_j}\,
    d\gamma_\sigma(a_j)\bigg)\,
    d\gamma_{j-1}
    \;=\; Z_{j-1}\;\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\big]\;,
\end{equation*}
where the inner integral is $e^{\Psi_j}$ by~\eqref{eq:Psi-def}, and the last step is the definition of $\mu_{j-1}$.  Iterating from $j=1$ to $\Lambda$ yields~\eqref{eq:telescope}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:good-geom}
There exist constants $c_0>0$, depending only on $d$, and $\kappa>0$, depending in addition on $\chi,g$ (neither depends on $j$ or $N_j$), such that the following holds. If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:width-gen}
    N_j \;\ge\; c_0\,(j{+}1)\,2^{2dj},
\end{equation}
then almost surely in $\omega$ there is a finite random index $j_0=j_0(\omega)\geq1$ such that for all $j\ge j_0$:
\begin{align}
    \sup_{x,y\in K}
    \big|\Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)-Q_j(x-y)\big|
    &\;\le\; 2^{-2j}\;,
    \label{eq:gg-approx}\\
    A_j^\omega \;:=\;
    \sup_{x\in K}\int g(y)\,
    |\Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)|\,dy
    &\;\le\; \kappa\,2^{-2j}\;.
    \label{eq:gg-off}
\end{align}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Fix $(x,y)\in K\times K$ and $j\geq1$. The quenched covariance~\eqref{eq:quenched-cov} is a sample mean of $N_j$ independent, identically distributed, bounded random variables:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gamma-sample}
    \Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)
    \;=\; \frac{1}{N_j}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_j} Z_\ell(x,y),
    \qquad
    Z_\ell(x,y) \;:=\; 2q_j\,
    \cos(b_{j,\ell}\cdot x+c_{j,\ell})\,
    \cos(b_{j,\ell}\cdot y+c_{j,\ell})\;.
\end{equation}
Each summand satisfies $|Z_\ell|\le 2q_j\le 2C\,2^{(d-2)j}$, and its expectation is $Q_j(x-y)$ by \Cref{lem:cov-mean}. By Hoeffding's inequality for bounded i.i.d.\ random variables, for each $\epsilon>0$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hoeffding}
    \Pr\!\big(|\Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)-Q_j(x-y)|>\epsilon\big)
    \;\le\; 2\exp\!\bigg(
    -\frac{N_j\,\epsilon^2}{8\,q_j^2}\bigg)
    \;\le\; 2\exp\!\bigg(
    -\frac{C\,N_j\,\epsilon^2}{2^{2(d-2)j}}\bigg)\;.
\end{equation}
The constant $C>0$ depends only on $d$ because the upper bound on $q_j$ in \eqref{eq:q_j-upper} rests only on $\chi\le1$, on the volume of the fixed annulus, and on $p^2+m^2\ge p^2$ in~\eqref{eq:Sj-bound}.

We upgrade the pointwise bound to a uniform bound over $K\times K$.  Differentiating either kernel with respect to $x$ or $y$ brings down a frequency bounded by $|b_{j,\ell}|\le 2^{j+1}$ for the cosines of~\eqref{eq:Gamma-sample}, and by $|p|\le 2^{j+1}$ on the support of $S_j$ for the integral \eqref{eq:Qj-kernel}.  Hence $\Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)$ and $Q_j(x-y)$, both viewed as functions of $(x,y)\in K\times K$, are Lipschitz in each spatial argument with constant
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lip}
    L \;=\; O(2^j\cdot q_j) \;=\; O(2^{(d-1)j})\;.
\end{equation}
Define $f:=\Gamma_j^\omega-Q_j$, which is Lipschitz in each argument with constant $2L$.  If $(x',y')$ is within Euclidean distance $\delta$ of $(x,y)$ in $\mathbb{R}^{2d}$, then, moving one argument at a time,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lip_estimate}
    |f(x,y)| \;\le\; |f(x',y')|
    + 2L\,\big(|x-x'|+|y-y'|\big)
    \;\le\; |f(x',y')| + 4L\delta\;.
\end{equation}
Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-choice}
    \delta \;:=\; \frac{2^{-2j}}{8L}\;,
\end{equation}
and choose a finite set of points in $K\times K$ whose $\delta$-balls cover it (a \emph{$\delta$-net}). If $|f|\le 2^{-2j}/2$ at every net point, then the Lipschitz estimate \eqref{eq:lip_estimate} gives $|f|\le 2^{-2j}/2+4L\delta=2^{-2j}$ everywhere on $K\times K$.

It remains to establish the bound at each net point. Since $K\times K$ is a bounded subset of $\mathbb{R}^{2d}$, such a net has $O(\delta^{-2d})=O(2^{2d(d+1)j})$ points.  Applying the Hoeffding bound~\eqref{eq:hoeffding} at each net point with $\epsilon=2^{-2j}/2$ and taking a union bound, the probability that the uniform bound fails is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:union}
    O\big(2^{2d(d+1)j}\big)\cdot
    \exp\!\big(-C\,N_j\cdot 2^{-2dj}\big)\;.
\end{equation}
With the width requirement~\eqref{eq:width-gen}, the bound~\eqref{eq:union} becomes
\begin{equation}
    O\big(2^{2d(d+1)j}\big)\cdot e^{-C\,c_0\,j}
    \;=\; O\big(\exp(C_{d}\,j - C\,c_0\,j)\big)\;,
\end{equation}
where $C_{d}:=2d(d{+}1)\ln 2$.  For $c_0$ large enough that $C\,c_0>C_d+1$, with $C$ the constant in~\eqref{eq:union}, this is $O(e^{-j})$ for all $j\ge1$. Since $\sum_{j\ge 1}e^{-j}<\infty$, by the Borel--Cantelli lemma there exists a.s.\ a finite $j_0$ such that \eqref{eq:gg-approx} holds for all $j\geq j_0$.

For the row integral bound, \eqref{eq:gg-approx} gives $|\Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)|\le|Q_j(x-y)|+2^{-2j}$ for $j\ge j_0$.  Integrating against $g(y)$ and taking the supremum over $x\in K$,
\begin{equation*}
    A_j^\omega
    \;\le\; A_j+2^{-2j}\,\|g\|_1
    \;\le\; \kappa\,2^{-2j}\;,
\end{equation*}
with $\kappa:=C+\|g\|_1$, where $C$ is the constant in $A_j\le C\,2^{-2j}$ (\Cref{lem:shell-dim}(d)).
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:nested}
For each shell $j$, fix a single infinite sequence $\omega_j^\infty=\big((b_{j,\ell},c_{j,\ell})\big)_{\ell\ge1}$ drawn i.i.d.\ from $\pi_j\otimes\mathrm{Unif}([-\pi,\pi])$ as in \eqref{eq:features}, all shells independent, and write $\omega^\infty:=(\omega_j^\infty)_{j\ge0}$.  For $n\in\mathbb{N}$ set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:width-schedule}
    N_j^{(n)} \;:=\; \big\lceil n\,c_0\,(j{+}1)\,2^{2dj}\big\rceil\;,
\end{equation}
with $c_0$ as in \Cref{thm:good-geom}, and let $\omega^{(n)}$ denote the truncation of $\omega^\infty$ in which shell $j$ uses the first $N_j^{(n)}$ features of $\omega_j^\infty$.  We write $\Gamma_j^{\omega^{(n)}}$, $q_j^{\omega^{(n)}}$, $D_\Lambda^{\omega^{(n)}}$, $\phi_\Lambda^{(n)}$, $\gamma_\Lambda^{(n)}$, $Z_\Lambda^{(n)}$, $V_\Lambda^{(n)}$, $\mu_\Lambda^{(n)}$ for objects built from $\omega^{(n)}$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:unif-gg}
Almost surely in $\omega^\infty$ there is a finite index $j_0^*=j_0^*(\omega^\infty)\geq1$ such that \eqref{eq:gg-approx} and \eqref{eq:gg-off} hold for every $j\ge j_0^*$ and every $n\ge1$ simultaneously, with the same constant $\kappa$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Call the pair $(j,n)$ a \emph{failure} if \eqref{eq:gg-approx} is violated at shell $j$ for the network of width $N_j^{(n)}$.  It is enough to control failures because the proof of \Cref{thm:good-geom} derives \eqref{eq:gg-off} from \eqref{eq:gg-approx}.  By the Borel--Cantelli lemma, it suffices that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:failure-sum}
    \sum_{j\ge1}\sum_{n\ge1}\Pr\big((j,n)\text{ is a failure}\big)\;<\;\infty\;,
\end{equation}
for then almost surely only finitely many pairs fail, and any $j_0^*$ exceeding every shell index in that finite set is as claimed.

The proof of \Cref{thm:good-geom} bounds the failure probability of shell $j$ at width $N$ by the cardinality of the $\delta$-net times the Hoeffding tail, and by the choice of $c_0$ there this is at most $O\big(2^{2d(d+1)j}\big)\exp\big(-(C_{d}+1)\,N\,2^{-2dj}/c_0\big)$.  From the width schedule~\eqref{eq:width-schedule},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:joint-BC}
    \Pr\big((j,n)\text{ is a failure}\big)
    \;\le\; C\,e^{C_{d}j}\,e^{-(C_{d}+1)\,n\,(j+1)}\;.
\end{equation}
At a fixed shell $j$, the bound~\eqref{eq:joint-BC} is geometric in $n$ with ratio $e^{-(C_{d}+1)(j+1)}\le e^{-1}$, and its sum over $n$ is therefore at most twice the $n=1$ term, $2C\,e^{-(C_{d}+1)}e^{-j}$.  The remaining sum over $j$ is geometric with ratio $e^{-1}$. Hence~\eqref{eq:failure-sum} holds.

Finally, the constant $\kappa$ of \Cref{thm:good-geom} depends on neither the realization nor the width, so the same value holds for every $n$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:kernel-conv}
Almost surely, for every shell $j$ and every compact set $K'\subset\mathbb{R}^d$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kernel-lim}
    \sup_{x,y\in K'}
    \big|\Gamma_j^{\omega^{(n)}}(x,y)-Q_j(x-y)\big|
    \;\longrightarrow\; 0
    \qquad(n\to\infty)\;,
\end{equation}
and consequently $\sup_{K'}|q_j^{\omega^{(n)}}-q_j|\to0$ and $\sup_{K'}|D_\Lambda^{\omega^{(n)}}-D_\Lambda|\to0$ for every $\Lambda$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Fix $j$ and a pair $(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^d\times\mathbb{R}^d$.  By \Cref{def:nested}, $\Gamma_j^{\omega^{(n)}}(x,y)$ is the average of the first $N_j^{(n)}$ terms of the sequence $\big(Z_\ell(x,y)\big)_{\ell\ge1}$ of~\eqref{eq:Gamma-sample}, and this sequence does not depend on $n$.  Its terms are i.i.d.\ and bounded, with mean $Q_j(x-y)$ by \Cref{lem:cov-mean}.  Because $N_j^{(n)}\to\infty$, the strong law of large numbers gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise-lim}
    \Gamma_j^{\omega^{(n)}}(x,y)\;\longrightarrow\;Q_j(x-y)
    \qquad\text{almost surely.}
\end{equation}
The event on which~\eqref{eq:pointwise-lim} holds depends on $(x,y)$, and there are uncountably many pairs.  On a bounded set, a $\delta$-net reduces them to finitely many, as in the proof of \Cref{thm:good-geom}. Write $f_n:=\Gamma_j^{\omega^{(n)}}-Q_j$, and let $K_R\subset\mathbb{R}^d$ be the closed ball of radius $R$ about the origin.  Both kernels are Lipschitz in each argument on all of $\mathbb{R}^d$ with the $n$-independent constant $L=O(2^{(d-1)j})$ of~\eqref{eq:lip}, so~\eqref{eq:lip_estimate} holds for every $f_n$ with the same $L$.  Fix $\epsilon>0$ and $R\in\mathbb{N}$, let $P$ be a finite $\delta$-net of $K_R\times K_R$ with $\delta:=\epsilon/(8L)$, and let $E_{j,\epsilon,R}$ be the event that~\eqref{eq:pointwise-lim} holds at every point of $P$.  As a finite intersection of probability-one events, $E_{j,\epsilon,R}$ has probability one.  On $E_{j,\epsilon,R}$ there is an $n_0$ such that $|f_n|\le\epsilon/2$ on $P$ for all $n\ge n_0$, and~\eqref{eq:lip_estimate} then gives $\sup_{K_R\times K_R}|f_n|\le\epsilon/2+4L\delta=\epsilon$ for these $n$.

The events $E_{j,1/k,R}$ with $j\ge0$ and $k,R\in\mathbb{N}$ are countably many, so their intersection $E$ has probability one.  On $E$, for every shell $j$ and every $R$, the supremum of $|f_n|$ over $K_R\times K_R$ is eventually at most $1/k$ for each $k$ and therefore tends to zero.  Every compact $K'$ lies in some $K_R$, which gives~\eqref{eq:kernel-lim}.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- notation-1: body_md -->
\label{not:quench}
From this point on, we fix a network whose widths satisfy~\eqref{eq:width-gen} and condition on a realization of its features $\omega=(\omega_j)_{j\ge 0}$ satisfying~\eqref{eq:gg-approx} and~\eqref{eq:gg-off} for all $j\ge j_0$.  Each network of \Cref{def:nested} is such a network, and \Cref{prop:uniform-N} applies \Cref{sec:d2-setup,sec:d2-converge} to the entire family.  In expectations and probabilities, we suppress the conditioning on the features and write, e.g.,  $\mathbb{E}_{a_j}[\,\cdot\,]$ for $\mathbb{E}_{a_j}[\,\cdot\mid\omega,\mathcal{F}_{j-1}]$.
<!-- end notation-1 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:T-coercivity}
Let $C\ge0$, $u\in\mathbb R$.  The Wick-ordered quartic obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:H4-coerce}
    H_4(u;C)\;\ge\;\tfrac12\,u^4 - 15\,C^2\;.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for all $B,z\in\mathbb{R}$, $D\ge0$, and $0\le q\le 2q_j$, the function $T$ of~\eqref{eq:T4-def} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:T-coercivity}
    T(B,D,q;\,z)\;\ge\;\tfrac{1}{16}\,z^4
    - c_{\mathrm{coer}}\,(1+B^4+D^2)\;,
\end{equation}
where $c_{\mathrm{coer}}$ depends only on $q_j$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
For~\eqref{eq:H4-coerce}, apply Young's inequality $6Cu^2\le\tfrac12 u^4+18C^2$ to the middle term of $H_4(u;C)=u^4-6Cu^2+3C^2$.

For \eqref{eq:T-coercivity}, the first term of \eqref{eq:T4-def} is bounded below by \eqref{eq:H4-coerce} with $u=B+z$ and $C=D+q$, and the second by $H_4(B;D)\le B^4+3D^2$, obtained by dropping its nonpositive middle term.  We also use the elementary inequality
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-sum}
    (x_1+\cdots+x_n)^p \;\le\; n^{p-1}\big(x_1^p+\cdots+x_n^p\big)\;,
    \qquad x_i\ge0,\ p\ge1\;,
\end{equation}
which is Jensen's inequality for the convex map $s\mapsto s^p$.  At $n=2$ and $p=4$, applied to $|z|\le|B+z|+|B|$, it gives $(B+z)^4\ge\tfrac18z^4-B^4$.  At $p=2$ it gives $(D+q)^2\le2D^2+8q_j^2$, since $q\le2q_j$.  Combining these bounds,
\begin{equation}
    T(B,D,q;z)\;\ge\;\tfrac{1}{16}z^4
    - \tfrac32 B^4 - 33D^2 - 120q_j^2\;,
\end{equation}
which is~\eqref{eq:T-coercivity} with $c_{\mathrm{coer}}:=\max\{33,\,120q_j^2\}$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:X-lower}
For each $j\ge1$ set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CB-def}
    C_B \;:=\; t\,c_{\mathrm{coer}}\int g(x)\,
    \Big(1+B_{j-1}(x)^4+\big(D^\omega_{j-1}(x)\big)^2\Big)\,dx\;,
\end{equation}
with $c_{\mathrm{coer}}$ as in \Cref{lem:T-coercivity}.  Then $C_B$ is finite and $\mathcal{F}_{j-1}$-measurable, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:X-bound}
    X_j \;\ge\; \frac{t}{16}\int g\,\varphi_j^4\,dx
    - C_B \;\ge\; -C_B
\end{equation}
for every $a_j\in\mathbb{R}^{N_j}$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
At each $x\in K$, write $B$, $D$, and $z$ for $B_{j-1}(x)$, $D_{j-1}^\omega(x)$, and $\varphi_j(x)$, and note that $q_j^\omega(x)\le 2q_j$.  Then $T$ obeys the coercivity bound~\eqref{eq:T-coercivity}.  Multiplying by $t$ and integrating against $g$ gives the first inequality of~\eqref{eq:X-bound}, and the second follows by discarding the quartic term, which is nonnegative.

The constant $C_B$ is $\mathcal{F}_{j-1}$-measurable because $B_{j-1}$ and $D^\omega_{j-1}$ are.  It is finite because both are continuous and hence bounded on the compact set $K$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:Psi-finite}
For each $j\ge1$, the shell log-Laplace transform~\eqref{eq:Psi-def} satisfies $0\le\Psi_j\le C_B$.  Moreover, $\mathbb{E}_{a_j}\big[\,|P(a_j)|\,e^{-X_j}\big]<\infty$ for every polynomial~$P$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
The lower bound is \Cref{lem:positivity}.  By \Cref{lem:X-lower}, $e^{-X_j}\le e^{C_B}$ for every $a_j$, and $C_B$ does not depend on $a_j$.  Hence $\mathbb{E}_{a_j}[e^{-X_j}]\le e^{C_B}$, which is the upper bound, and $\mathbb{E}_{a_j}[\,|P(a_j)|\,e^{-X_j}]\le e^{C_B}\,\mathbb{E}_{a_j}[\,|P(a_j)|\,]$, which is finite because the Gaussian prior has moments of all orders.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:Schur}
For all $j\ge j_0$ (\Cref{thm:good-geom}),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Schur}
    \|\Sigma_j\|_{\mathrm{op}}
    \;\le\; \frac{A_j^\omega}{\sigma^2}
    \;\le\; \frac{\kappa\,2^{-2j}}{\sigma^2}\,.
\end{equation}
The first inequality holds for every realization of the features, and only the second uses~\eqref{eq:gg-off}.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The matrix $\Sigma_j$ is symmetric and positive semidefinite, since $v^T\Sigma_j v=\int g\,(U_j(x)^T\,v)^2\,dx\ge0$, so its operator norm is its largest eigenvalue $\lambda$.  Let $v$ be a corresponding eigenvector, and define the associated field $f(x):=U_j(x)^T\,v$.  Pairing the eigenvalue equation $\Sigma_j v=\lambda v$ with $U_j(x)$ and using $U_j(x)^T\,U_j(y)=\Gamma^\omega_j(x,y)/\sigma^2$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:schur-eigen}
    \lambda\,f(x)
    \;=\; U_j(x)^T\,\Sigma_jv
    \;=\; \frac{1}{\sigma^2}\int g(y)\,\Gamma^\omega_j(x,y)\,f(y)\,dy\;.
\end{equation}
If $f$ vanishes identically on $K$, then $\lambda|v|^2=\int g\,f^2\,dx=0$, and the bound is trivial. Otherwise, $|f|$ is continuous on the compact set $K$ and attains its maximum at some $x_*\in K$ with $|f(x_*)|>0$.  Evaluating \eqref{eq:schur-eigen} at $x_*$ and bounding $|f(y)|\le|f(x_*)|$ under the integral,
\begin{equation*}
    \lambda\,|f(x_*)|
    \;\le\; \frac{|f(x_*)|}{\sigma^2}
    \sup_{x\in K}\int g(y)\,|\Gamma^\omega_j(x,y)|\,dy
    \;=\; |f(x_*)|\,\frac{A_j^\omega}{\sigma^2}\;.
\end{equation*}
Dividing by $|f(x_*)|$ gives $\|\Sigma_j\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\lambda\le A_j^\omega/\sigma^2$.

This argument is the continuum form of the fact that no eigenvalue of a matrix exceeds its largest absolute row sum.  For a symmetric kernel, it gives the same bound as the Schur test \cite[Thm.~5.2]{HalmosSunder1978}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:hessian}
Set $\mathcal{D}_j:=\sup_{x\in K}D^\omega_j(x)$.  For every $j\ge j_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hessian-combined}
    \nabla^2_{a_j}W_j
    \;\ge\; \frac{1}{\sigma^2}\Big(1
    - \underbrace{12\,t\,\kappa\,\mathcal{D}_j
    \cdot 2^{-2j}}_{\text{one-shell perturbation}}
    \Big)\,I_{N_j}\;.
\end{equation}
The one-shell perturbation depends on neither $\Lambda$ nor the coefficients $a_0,\dots,a_j$, and with constants free of the widths $(N_j)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:quartic-pert-size}
    12\,t\,\kappa\,\mathcal{D}_j\,2^{-2j}
    \;=\;
    \begin{cases}
    O\big(t\,2^{-2j}j\big) & d=2\;,\\[4pt]
    O\big(t\,2^{(d-4)j}\big) & d\ge3\;.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Differentiating~\eqref{eq:W-def} twice in $a_j$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hessian}
    \nabla^2_{a_j}W_j
    \;=\; \frac{1}{\sigma^2}\,I_{N_j}
    + t\int g(x)\,
    T_{zz}\,U_j(x)\,U_j(x)^T\,dx\;,
\end{equation}
where $T_{zz}$ is the second $z$-derivative of $T$ \eqref{eq:T4-def}, evaluated at $B=B_{j-1}(x)$, $D=D^\omega_{j-1}(x)$, $q=q^\omega_j(x)$, and $z=\varphi_j(x)$. Direct differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Tzz4}
    T_{zz} \;=\; 12(B+z)^2 - 12(D+q)\;.
\end{equation}
The first term of~\eqref{eq:Tzz4} is nonnegative, and the second is at least $-12\big(D^\omega_{j-1}(x)+q^\omega_j(x)\big)=-12\,D^\omega_j(x)\ge-12\,\mathcal{D}_j$.  The quartic contribution to the Hessian is therefore bounded below by $-12\,t\,\mathcal{D}_j\,\Sigma_j$. Using \Cref{lem:Schur},
\begin{align}
    \nabla^2_{a_j}W_j
    &\;\ge\; \frac{1}{\sigma^2}\,I
    - 12\,t\,\mathcal{D}_j\,\|\Sigma_j\|_{\mathrm{op}} I
    \notag\\
    &\;\ge\; \frac{1}{\sigma^2}\Big(1
    - 12\,t\,\kappa\,\mathcal{D}_j\cdot 2^{-2j}
    \Big)\,I\;.
\end{align}

Since $q^\omega_j\le 2q_j$ deterministically, $\mathcal{D}_j\le 2D_j$, so \Cref{lem:shell-dim}(a),(e) give a constant $c_{\mathcal{D}}$, depending only on $d$, $\chi$, and $m$, with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Dcal-bound}
    \mathcal{D}_j \;\le\;
    \begin{cases}
    c_{\mathcal{D}}\,(j{+}1) & d=2\;,\\[4pt]
    c_{\mathcal{D}}\,2^{(d-2)j} & d\ge3\;,
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}
and multiplying by $12\,t\,\kappa\,2^{-2j}$ gives~\eqref{eq:quartic-pert-size}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:BL}
Let $2\le d<4$.  With no restriction on the coupling, there is a threshold $j_1$ with $j_0\le j_1<\infty$ such that for every $j\ge j_1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:BL-hessian}
    \nabla^2_{a_j}W_j
    \;\ge\; \frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\,I_{N_j}\;.
\end{equation}
The threshold depends only on $d$, $\chi$, $m$, $t$, $\kappa$, and the threshold $j_0$ of \Cref{thm:good-geom}, and in particular not on the widths $(N_j)$, on $\Lambda$, or on the coefficients $a_0,\dots,a_j$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
By~\eqref{eq:quartic-pert-size} the one-shell perturbation is at most $12\,t\,\kappa c_{\mathcal{D}}(j{+}1)2^{-2j}$ at $d=2$ and $12\,t\,\kappa c_{\mathcal{D}}2^{-j}$ at $d=3$.  Both bounds are free of the widths and tend to zero, so choose $j_1\ge j_0$ beyond which the perturbation is at most $1/2$.  \Cref{prop:hessian} then gives $\nabla^2_{a_j}W_j\ge\sigma^{-2}(1-\tfrac12)I=\tfrac{1}{2\sigma^2}I$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:tilted-moments}
Let $2\le d<4$.  Under $\rho_j$, write $\bar{\varphi}_j(x) :=\langle\varphi_j(x)\rangle$ for the tilted mean and $\tilde{\varphi}_j(x) :=\varphi_j(x)-\bar{\varphi}_j(x)$ for the centered part.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:centered-moments}
    \big\langle\tilde{\varphi}_j(x)^{2k}
    \big\rangle
    \;\le\; \big(8q_j^\omega(x)\big)^k\,k!\,
\end{equation}
for all $j\ge j_1$, $k\ge 1$, and $x\in K$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
By the Hessian bound~\eqref{eq:BL-hessian} and the Bakry--\'Emery criterion (\cite{BakryEmery1985}; \cite[Theorem~5.2]{ledoux2001concentration}), $\rho_j$ satisfies the logarithmic Sobolev inequality
\begin{equation}\label{eq:LSI}
    \mathrm{Ent}_{\rho_j}(h^2)
    \;\le\; 4\sigma^2\int|\nabla h|^2\,
    d\rho_j\;,
\end{equation}
for every smooth $h:\mathbb R^{N_j}\to\mathbb R$, where $\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(h):=\int h\log h\,d\mu -\big(\!\int h\,d\mu\big)\log\!\big(\!\int h\,d\mu\big)$.

The shell field $\varphi_j(x)=U_j(x)^T\,a_j$ is a linear functional of $a_j$ with gradient $U_j(x)$, hence Lipschitz constant
\begin{equation}
    L:=|U_j(x)|\;,
    \qquad
    L^2=|U_j(x)|^2
    =\frac{1}{\sigma^2}\,q_j^\omega(x)\;.
\end{equation}
If $L=0$ (that is, $q_j^\omega(x)=0$) then $\varphi_j(x)=0$ almost surely, and the centered moments vanish. Assume henceforth $L>0$.  Applying the Herbst argument \cite[Theorem~5.3]{ledoux2001concentration} to the $1$-Lipschitz functionals $\pm\varphi_j(x)/L$ and taking a union bound over the two tails gives, for every $s\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:herbst-tail}
    \rho_j\big(
    |\varphi_j(x)-\bar\varphi_j(x)|>s\big)
    \;\le\; 2\,e^{-s^2/(2\tau_j^2)}\;,
    \qquad
    \tau_j^2:=2\sigma^2L^2=2\,q_j^\omega(x)\;.
\end{equation}
With the tail bound~\eqref{eq:herbst-tail}, the layer-cake formula gives, for $k\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
    \big\langle\tilde\varphi_j(x)^{2k}\big\rangle
    = 2k\int_0^\infty \,s^{2k-1}\,
      \rho_j\big(|\tilde\varphi_j(x)|>s\big)\,ds
    \le 4k\!\int_0^\infty\! s^{2k-1}e^{-s^2/(2\tau_j^2)}\,ds
    = 2^{k+1}k!\,\tau_j^{2k}\;.
\end{equation}
Inserting $\tau_j^2=2\,q_j^\omega(x)$ gives $2^{k+1}k!\,\tau_j^{2k}=2\cdot4^k\,k!\,q^\omega_j(x)^k$, and $2\cdot4^k\le8^k$ for $k\ge1$ yields~\eqref{eq:centered-moments}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:mean-exact}
For every $j\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mean-exact}
    \bar{\varphi}_j(x)
    = -4t\int g(y)\,\Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)\,
    \big\langle H_3\big(B_j(y);D^\omega_j(y)\big)
    \big\rangle\,dy \;.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Write $\partial_\ell:=\partial/\partial a_{j,\ell}$.  Since $X_j\ge-C_B$ by~\eqref{eq:X-bound}, $e^{-W_j}\le e^{C_B}e^{-|a_j|^2/(2\sigma^2)}$, and $\partial_\ell W_j$ is a polynomial in $a_j$.  Integration by parts therefore gives $\int\partial_\ell(e^{-W_j})\,da_j=0$, that is, $\langle\partial_\ell W_j\rangle=0$.
The gradient is
\begin{equation*}
    \partial_\ell W_j=\frac{a_{j,\ell}}{\sigma^2}
    +4t\int g(y)\,H_3\big(B_j(y);D^\omega_j(y)\big)\,U_{j,\ell}(y)\,dy\;,
\end{equation*}
so averaging and solving for $\bar a_{j,\ell}:=\langle a_{j,\ell} \rangle$ gives
\begin{equation*}
    \bar a_{j,\ell}
    =-4t\,\sigma^2\int g(y)\,\big\langle
    H_3\big(B_j(y);D^\omega_j(y)\big)\big\rangle\,
    U_{j,\ell}(y)\,dy\;.
\end{equation*}
The average passes inside the $y$-integral by Fubini, because on $K$ the cubic $H_3\big(B_j(y);D^\omega_j(y)\big)$ is bounded by a polynomial in $|a_j|$ and $\rho_j$ has moments of all orders (\Cref{lem:Psi-finite}).  Since $\varphi_j(x)$ is linear in the coefficients, $\bar\varphi_j(x)=\sum_\ell U_{j,\ell}(x)\,\bar a_{j,\ell}$.  Substituting $\bar a_{j,\ell}$ and $\sigma^2\sum_\ell U_{j,\ell}(x)\,U_{j,\ell}(y)=\Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)$ yields~\eqref{eq:mean-exact}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:mean-shift}
Set
\begin{equation*}
    R_j \;:=\; 1+\sup_{y\in K}\Big(B_{j-1}(y)^2
    +D_j^\omega(y)\Big)\;,
    \qquad
    \epsilon_j \;:=\; 4t\,A_j^\omega\;,
\end{equation*}
so that $\epsilon_j\le4t\,\kappa\,2^{-2j}$ by~\eqref{eq:gg-off} once $j\geq j_0$.  For $j\ge j_1$, if the smallness condition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:smallness-cond}
    \epsilon_j\,R_j^{3/2}\le\tfrac{1}{512}
\end{equation}
holds, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mean-shift}
    \sup_{x\in K}\big|\bar\varphi_{j,\alpha}(x)\big|\;\le\;\tfrac12\;,
\end{equation}
for all $\alpha\in[0,1]$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Abbreviate $\bar\varphi:=\bar\varphi_{j,\alpha}$, $\tilde\varphi:=\tilde\varphi_{j,\alpha}$, $\delta:=D_{j-1}^\omega+\alpha q_j^\omega \le D_j^\omega$, and $s:=\sup_{x\in K}|\bar\varphi(x)|$.

Taking absolute values in the mean identity~\eqref{eq:mean-alpha}, bounding $\int g(y)\,|\Gamma^\omega_j(x,y)|\,dy$ by $A^\omega_j$, and using $\alpha\le1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:S-ineq}
    s\le 4t\,A_j^\omega\;\sup_{y\in K}
    \big|\big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(y)
    \big\rangle_\alpha\big|\;.
\end{equation}
Split the field into its mean and centered parts, $B_{j-1}+\varphi_{j,\alpha}=\beta+\tilde\varphi$, with $\beta:=B_{j-1}+\bar\varphi$ and $\langle\tilde\varphi\rangle_\alpha=0$. Expanding $H_3^{(\alpha)}$ about $\beta$ and averaging,
\begin{equation*}
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}\big\rangle_\alpha
    =H_3(\beta;\delta)+3\beta\,\langle\tilde\varphi^2
    \rangle_\alpha+\langle\tilde\varphi^3\rangle_\alpha
    =\mathcal{S}(y)+\mathcal{R}(y)\;,
\end{equation*}
where $\mathcal S(y)$ is the value at $\bar\varphi=0$,
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}(y):=H_3(B_{j-1};\delta)
    +3B_{j-1}\,\langle\tilde\varphi^2\rangle_\alpha
    +\langle\tilde\varphi^3\rangle_\alpha\;,
\end{equation*}
and $\mathcal R(y)$ is the remainder.  Since $H_3(B{+}\bar\varphi;\delta)-H_3(B;\delta) =3H_2(B;\delta)\bar\varphi+3B\bar\varphi^2+\bar\varphi^3$,
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{R}(y)=3H_2(B_{j-1};\delta)\,\bar\varphi
    +3B_{j-1}\,\bar\varphi^2+\bar\varphi^3
    +3\langle\tilde\varphi^2\rangle_\alpha\,\bar\varphi\;.
\end{equation*}

Set $P_j:=\sup_{y\in K}\big(1+|B_{j-1}(y)|^3+D^\omega_j(y)\,|B_{j-1}(y)|+(q^\omega_j(y))^{3/2}\big)$.  For $\mathcal S(y)$, \Cref{cor:tilted-moments} gives $\langle\tilde\varphi^2\rangle_\alpha\le 8q_j^\omega$ and $|\langle\tilde\varphi^3\rangle_\alpha| \le\langle\tilde\varphi^2\rangle_\alpha^{1/2} \langle\tilde\varphi^4\rangle_\alpha^{1/2} \le32\,(q_j^\omega)^{3/2}$. These bounds, together with $|H_3(B;\delta)|\le|B|^3+3\delta|B|$ and $\delta,q^\omega_j\le D_j^\omega$, give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:source-bd}
    |\mathcal{S}(y)|\le 32\,P_j\;.
\end{equation}
The remainder is a polynomial in $\bar\varphi$ with no constant term, and its coefficients are bounded by multiples of $R_j$, namely $|3H_2(B;\delta)|\le3(B^2{+}\delta)\le3R_j$, $3\langle\tilde\varphi^2\rangle_\alpha\le24R_j$, and $|B_{j-1}|\le R_j^{1/2}\le R_j$.  To bound the powers of $\bar\varphi$ by $s$ itself, we assume for now that $s\le1$, and remove this assumption at the end of the proof.  Then $\bar\varphi^2,|\bar\varphi|^3\le s$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:feedback-bd}
    |\mathcal{R}(y)|\le 31\,R_j\,s\;.
\end{equation}

Insert \eqref{eq:source-bd}--\eqref{eq:feedback-bd} into~\eqref{eq:S-ineq}:
\begin{equation*}
    s\le\epsilon_j\big(32\,P_j+31\,R_j\,s\big)\;.
\end{equation*}
Assume that \eqref{eq:smallness-cond} holds.  Since $R_j\ge1$, the condition gives $31\,\epsilon_jR_j\le31\,\epsilon_jR_j^{3/2}\le\tfrac{31}{512}<\tfrac12$, and hence $s\le64\,\epsilon_jP_j$.  Since also $|B|\le R_j^{1/2}$ and $q_j^\omega\le D_j^\omega\le R_j$, each of the four terms of $P_j$ is at most $R_j^{3/2}$, so $P_j\le4R_j^{3/2}$, and the smallness condition gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:S-half}
    s\;\le\;256\,\epsilon_jR_j^{3/2}\;\le\;\tfrac{256}{512}\;=\;\tfrac12\;.
\end{equation}
This holds for every $\alpha$ with $s(\alpha)\le1$, and it remains to show that $s(\alpha)\le1$ for every $\alpha\in[0,1]$.

The map $\alpha\mapsto s(\alpha)$ is continuous on $[0,1]$.  Each coefficient mean $\langle a_{j,\ell}\rangle_\alpha$ is a ratio of two integrals with positive denominator $e^{\Psi_j(\alpha)}$, and both integrals are continuous in $\alpha$ by dominated convergence, with a dominating function uniform on $[0,1]$ because $X_{j,\alpha}\ge-C_B$.  Hence $\bar\varphi_{j,\alpha}=\sqrt{\alpha}\sum_\ell U_{j,\ell}\,\langle a_{j,\ell}\rangle_\alpha$, a finite sum of functions bounded on $K$, is continuous in $\alpha$ in the supremum norm on $K$, and so is its norm $s(\alpha)$.

Let $\mathcal{I}:=\{\alpha\in[0,1]:s(\alpha)\le1\}$.  It is nonempty because $s(0)=0$, and closed in $[0,1]$ as the preimage of $(-\infty,1]$ under the continuous map $s$.  By~\eqref{eq:S-half}, $s\le\tfrac12$ on $\mathcal{I}$, so $\mathcal{I}$ coincides with $s^{-1}((-\infty,1))$ and is open in $[0,1]$ as well.  Since $[0,1]$ is connected and $\mathcal I$ is a nonempty clopen subset of it, $\mathcal{I}=[0,1]$. Therefore, \eqref{eq:S-half} holds for every $\alpha\in[0,1]$, which is~\eqref{eq:mean-shift}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:interp}
For every $\alpha\in[0,1]$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:psi-prime}
    \Psi_j'(\alpha)
    \;=\; 8t^2\iint g(x)\,g(y)\,
    \Gamma_j^\omega(x,y)\,
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)\,
    H_3^{(\alpha)}(y)\big\rangle_\alpha
    \,dx\,dy\;.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:d2-good-field}
Set $c_1:=\big(6144\sqrt3\,t\,\kappa\big)^{-1/3}$, and define the $\mathcal{F}_{j-1}$-measurable good-field event:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:good-field}
    G_j \;:=\; \Big\{(a_0,\dots,a_{j-1}):\,\sup_{x\in K}\big|B_{j-1}(x)\big|
    \;\le\; c_1\,2^{2j/3}\,\Big\}\;.
\end{equation}
There exists $j_2\ge j_1$, depending only on $t$, $\kappa$, $c_q$, and $j_1$, such that for all $j\ge j_2$ and every $(a_0,\dots,a_{j-1})\in G_j$, the smallness condition~\eqref{eq:smallness-cond} holds.  The mean shift bound~\eqref{eq:mean-shift} then applies.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
On $G_j$, using $D^\omega_j\le c_q\,(j{+}1)$,
\begin{equation}
    R_j \;\le\; 1+c_1^22^{4j/3}+c_q\,(j{+}1)\;.
\end{equation}
By~\eqref{eq:power-sum} at $n=3$, $p=3/2$, and $\epsilon_j\le4t\kappa 2^{-2j}$,
\begin{equation}
    \epsilon_j\,R_j^{3/2}
    \;\le\; 4\sqrt3\,t\kappa\Big(2^{-2j}
    + c_1^3 + \big(c_q (j{+}1)\big)^{3/2}\,2^{-2j}\Big)\;.
\end{equation}
Choose $j_2\ge j_1$ so large that for $j\ge j_2$ each of the two $j$-dependent terms in the bracket is at most $c_1^3$.  Then $\epsilon_j R_j^{3/2}\le 12\sqrt3\,t\kappa\,c_1^3=\tfrac{1}{512}$.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:d2-tilted}
There is a constant $C_H>0$ such that for all $j\ge j_2$, $\alpha\in[0,1]$, $(a_0,\dots,a_{j-1})\in G_j$, and $x\in K$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-tilted-bound}
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)^2
    \big\rangle_\alpha
    \;\le\; C_H\,\big(1+B_{j-1}(x)^6
    +D^\omega_{j-1}(x)^3\big)\;.
\end{equation}
The constant $C_H$ depends only on $c_q$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Expand via $\beta:=B_{j-1}(x)+\bar\varphi_{j,\alpha}(x)$, $\delta:=D^\omega_{j-1}(x)+\alpha q^\omega_j(x)$, and $\tilde\varphi:=\tilde\varphi_{j,\alpha}(x)$, as in the proof of \Cref{lem:mean-shift}:
\begin{equation}
    H_3^{(\alpha)}(x) \;=\;
    \tilde{\varphi}^3
    + 3\beta\,\tilde{\varphi}^2
    + (3\beta^2-3\delta)\,\tilde{\varphi}
    + (\beta^3-3\delta\beta).
\end{equation}
Squaring via~\eqref{eq:power-sum} at $n=4$, $p=2$, and taking $\langle\cdot\rangle_\alpha$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-H3-sq}
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)^2
    \big\rangle_\alpha
    \;\le\; 4\Big(
    \langle\tilde{\varphi}^6\rangle_\alpha
    + 9\beta^2\,\langle\tilde{\varphi}^4\rangle_\alpha
    + 9(\beta^2{+}\delta)^2\,
    \langle\tilde{\varphi}^2\rangle_\alpha
    + (\beta^3{+}3\delta|\beta|)^2\Big)\;.
\end{equation}
Bounding the centered tilted moments by \eqref{eq:d2-cent} gives
\begin{equation}
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)^2\big\rangle_\alpha
    \;\le\; C_1\,\big(1+\beta^2+\beta^4+\beta^2\delta
    +\delta^2+\beta^6+\beta^4\delta+\beta^2\delta^2\big)\;.
\end{equation}
Every monomial $\beta^a\delta^b$ on the right has $a$ even and $a+2b\le6$.  Young's inequality for three factors, with weights $\tfrac{a}{6}$, $\tfrac{b}{3}$, and $1-\tfrac{a}{6}-\tfrac{b}{3}\ge0$, then gives
\begin{equation}
    \beta^a\delta^b
    \;\le\; \tfrac{a}{6}\,\beta^6+\tfrac{b}{3}\,\delta^3
    +\Big(1-\tfrac{a}{6}-\tfrac{b}{3}\Big)
    \;\le\; 1+\beta^6+\delta^3\;.
\end{equation}
Therefore,
\begin{equation}
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)^2\big\rangle_\alpha
    \;\le\; C_2\,\big(1+\beta^6+\delta^3\big)\;.
\end{equation}

It remains to replace $\beta$ by $B:=B_{j-1}(x)$ and $\delta$ by $D:=D^\omega_{j-1}(x)$.  On $G_j$, \Cref{lem:d2-good-field} gives $|\bar{\varphi}_{j,\alpha}(x)|\le\tfrac12$ for all $x$ and $\alpha$, so
\begin{equation*}
    |\beta|^6
    \;=\; \big|B+\bar{\varphi}_{j,\alpha}(x)\big|^6
    \;\le\; 2^5\big(|B|^6+1\big)\;,
\end{equation*}
while $\delta=D+\alpha\,q^\omega_j(x)\le D+c_q$ gives
\begin{equation*}
    \delta^3 \;\le\; (D+c_q)^3 \;\le\; 4\big(D^3+c_q^3\big)\;.
\end{equation*}
Therefore $1+\beta^6+\delta^3\le C_3\big(1+B^6+D^3\big)$, which is~\eqref{eq:d2-tilted-bound} with $C_H:=C_2C_3$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:d2-one-shell}
There is a constant $C_\star>0$ such that for all $j\ge j_2$ and $(a_0,\dots,a_{j-1})\in G_j$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-one-shell}
    0 \;\le\; \Psi_j(1) \;\le\;
    C_\star\,2^{-2j}\int g(x)\,
    \big(1+B_{j-1}(x)^6+D^\omega_{j-1}(x)^3\big)\,dx\;.
\end{equation}
The constant $C_\star$ depends only on $t$, $\kappa$, and $C_H$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
The lower bound is \Cref{lem:positivity}.  For the upper bound, integrate the interpolation derivative~\eqref{eq:psi-prime}:
\begin{equation}
    \Psi_j(1) \;=\; \int_0^1\!\Psi_j'(\alpha)\,d\alpha
    \;=\; 8t^2\int_0^1\!\iint g(x)\,g(y)\,
    \Gamma^\omega_j(x,y)\,\big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)\,
    H_3^{(\alpha)}(y)\big\rangle_\alpha
    \,dx\,dy\,d\alpha\;.
\end{equation}
By Cauchy--Schwarz under $\langle\cdot\rangle_\alpha$, followed by AM--GM:
\begin{equation}
    \big|\big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)\,
    H_3^{(\alpha)}(y)\big\rangle_\alpha\big|
    \;\le\; \sqrt{\big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)^2
    \big\rangle_\alpha\,
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(y)^2
    \big\rangle_\alpha}
    \;\le\; \tfrac{1}{2}\Big(
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)^2
    \big\rangle_\alpha
    +\big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(y)^2
    \big\rangle_\alpha\Big)\;.
\end{equation}
Substituting, and using the symmetry $\Gamma^\omega_j(x,y)=\Gamma^\omega_j(y,x)$,
\begin{align}
    \Psi_j(1)
    &\;\le\; 8t^2\int_0^1\!\int g(x)\,
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)^2
    \big\rangle_\alpha\,
    \bigg(\int g(y)\,|\Gamma^\omega_j(x,y)|\,dy\bigg)
    dx\,d\alpha
    \notag\\
    &\;\le\; 8t^2\,A_j^\omega\int_0^1\!\int g(x)\,
    \big\langle H_3^{(\alpha)}(x)^2
    \big\rangle_\alpha\,dx\,d\alpha\;.
\end{align}
Applying the second-moment bound (\Cref{thm:d2-tilted}) and $A_j^\omega\le\kappa\,2^{-2j}$:
\begin{equation}
    \Psi_j(1) \;\le\;
    8t^2\kappa C_H\,2^{-2j}\int g(x)\,
    \big(1+B_{j-1}(x)^6+D^\omega_{j-1}(x)^3\big)\,dx\;,
\end{equation}
which is~\eqref{eq:d2-one-shell} with $C_\star:=8t^2\kappa C_H$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:d2-sup-tail}
There are constants $c_2,C_K>0$ such that for all $J\ge1$ and all $s>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-sup-tail}
    \gamma_{J-1}\Big(\sup_{x\in K}\big|B_{J-1}(x)\big|>s\Big)
    \;\le\; C_K\,16^{J}
    \exp\!\Big(-\frac{c_2\,s^2}{c_qJ}\Big)\;.
\end{equation}
The constant $c_2$ is universal, and $C_K$ depends only on $K$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:d2-bad-prob}
There are a constant $c_3>0$ and a threshold $j_3\ge1$, depending only on $t$, $g$, $\kappa$, and $c_q$, such that for all $j\ge j_3$
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-bad-prob}
    \gamma_j\big(\mathcal{G}_j^c\big)
    \;\le\; C_K\exp\Big(-\frac{c_3\,2^{2j/3}}{j}\Big),
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal{G}_j$ as in~\eqref{eq:fJ-def} and $C_K$ as in \Cref{lem:d2-sup-tail}.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
The event $\mathcal{G}_j^c=\{f_j>1\}\cup G_j^c$ is $\mathcal{F}_{j-1}$-measurable, and because the marginal of $\gamma_j$ on the first $j$ shells is $\gamma_{j-1}$, we have $\gamma_j(\mathcal{G}_j^c)=\gamma_{j-1}(\mathcal{G}_j^c)$.

Take $j_3$ large enough that for $j\geq j_3$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-fj-const}
    C_\star\,\|g\|_1\big(1+(c_qj)^3\big)\,2^{-2j}\;\le\;\tfrac12\;.
\end{equation}
The field-independent part of $f_j$ is then at most $\tfrac12$, so $f_j>1$ forces $C_\star2^{-2j}\!\int g\,B_{j-1}^6\,dy>\tfrac12$. Bounding the integral by $\|g\|_1\big(\sup_K|B_{j-1}|\big)^6$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \sup_K|B_{j-1}|\;>\;C_1\,2^{j/3},
    \qquad C_1:=\big(2C_\star\|g\|_1\big)^{-1/6},
\end{equation*}
and $G_j^c$ forces $\sup_K|B_{j-1}|>c_1\,2^{2j/3}$ by~\eqref{eq:good-field}.  After enlarging $j_3$ so that $c_1 2^{2j/3}\ge C_1\,2^{j/3}$, both parts lie in $\{\sup_K|B_{j-1}|>C_1\,2^{j/3}\}$, and \Cref{lem:d2-sup-tail} at index $j$ and threshold $s=C_1\,2^{j/3}$ gives
\begin{equation*}
    \gamma_{j-1}\big(\mathcal{G}_j^c\big)
    \;\le\; C_K\,16^{j}
    \exp\!\Big(-\frac{c_2C_1^2\,2^{2j/3}}{c_qj}\Big)\;.
\end{equation*}
Enlarging $j_3$ once more so that $16^{j}\le\exp\big(c_2C_1^22^{2j/3}/(2c_qj)\big)$, which holds for large $j$ because $2^{2j/3}$ outgrows $j^2$, gives~\eqref{eq:d2-bad-prob} with $c_3:=c_2C_1^2/(2c_q)$.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:d2-moments}
There are constants $C_M,\mathcal{K}>0$ such that for all $J\ge0$, $p\ge1$, and $x\in K$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-moments}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}\big[B_J(x)^{2p}\big]
    \;\le\; \mathcal{K}\,C_M^{\,p}\,p!\,(J{+}1)^p\;.
\end{equation}
The growth constant $C_M$ depends only on $c_q$, and the prefactor $\mathcal{K}$ depends only on $t$, $g$, $c_q$, $\kappa$, and the threshold $j_4$ of~\eqref{eq:j4}.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Throughout the proof, we use $(p!)^{1/p}\in[p/e,\,p]$ repeatedly.

The constants are fixed in the following order.  First,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-Cg}
    C_M \;:=\; 512\,e\,c_q\;,
\end{equation}
so that $C_M$ depends only on $c_q$.  Next, with $c_V$ as in~\eqref{eq:d2-C1}, let $J_\star\ge j_4$ be the finite threshold determined by conditions \eqref{eq:d2-Jstar-a}--\eqref{eq:d2-Jstar-c} of Step 5, which depends only on $t$, $g$, $\kappa$, $c_q$, and $j_4$. Finally,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-K}
    \mathcal{K} \;:=\; e^{c_V(J_\star+1)^2}\;,
\end{equation}
so that $\mathcal{K}\ge1$.  With $A_p:=C_M^{\,p}\,p!$, the claim~\eqref{eq:d2-moments} reads $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}[B_J(x)^{2p}]\le\mathcal{K}A_p(J+1)^p$.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Step 0: direct bound.}
For every $J\ge0$, $p\ge1$, and $x\in K$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-direct}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}\big[B_J(x)^{2p}\big]
    \;\le\; e^{c_V(J+1)^2}\;p!\,
    \big(2c_q(J{+}1)\big)^p\;.
\end{equation}
Indeed, $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}[F]=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma_J}[e^{-V_J}F]/Z_J$ with $Z_J\ge1$ by \Cref{lem:V-lower}, and $e^{-V_J}\le e^{c_V(J+1)^2}$ pointwise by~\eqref{eq:d2-C1}.  Under $\gamma_J$ the variable $B_J(x)$ is $\mathcal{N}(0,D^\omega_J(x))$, and $(2p{-}1)!!\le 2^p p!$.  Consequently \eqref{eq:d2-moments} holds:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item for all $p\ge1$ whenever $J\le J_\star$, by the
choice~\eqref{eq:d2-K} of $\mathcal{K}$ and $C_M>2c_q$. This covers the base case $J=0$ and the finitely many shells below $J_\star$ without induction;
\item for $J>J_\star$ whenever
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-p1}
    p \;\ge\; p_1(J) \;:=\;
    \frac{c_V\,(J{+}1)^2}{\ln\!\big(C_M/(2c_q)\big)}\;,
\end{equation}
without using $\mathcal{K}$ at all.
\end{enumerate}
It therefore suffices to carry out the inductive step at pairs $(J,p)$ with $J>J_\star$ and $p<p_1(J)$, assuming \eqref{eq:d2-moments} at level $J-1$ for all orders in $p$.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Step 1: reduction and split.}
Fix $x\in K$.  Throughout Steps~1--4 we abbreviate $B(x):=B_{J-1}(x)$ and $D(x):=D^\omega_{J-1}(x)$.  Since $V_J=V_{J-1}+X_J$ and $\gamma_J=\gamma_{J-1}\otimes\gamma_\sigma(a_J)$, integrating over $a_J$ first and dividing by~\eqref{eq:telescope-step} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-ratio}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}\big[B_J(x)^{2p}\big]
    \;=\; \frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_J}\,
    \big\langle(B(x)+\varphi_J(x))^{2p}\big\rangle\big]} 
    {\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_J}\big]}\;,
\end{equation}
where $\langle\cdot\rangle$ denotes the tilted expectation over $a_J$.  Since $\Psi_J\ge 0$ (\Cref{lem:positivity}), the denominator is at least~$1$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-drop-denom}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}\big[B_J(x)^{2p}\big]
    \;\le\; \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_J}\,\mathcal{M}_p\big]\;,
    \qquad \mathcal{M}_p:=\big\langle(B(x)+\varphi_J(x))^{2p}
    \big\rangle\;\ge\;0\;.
\end{equation}
With $f_J$ and $\mathcal{G}_J$ as in~\eqref{eq:fJ-def}, we have $0\le\Psi_J\le f_J\le1$ on $\mathcal{G}_J$. Hence $e^{\Psi_J}\le1+2f_J$ there, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-split}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_J}\mathcal{M}_p\big]
    \;\le\; \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    \;+\; 2\,\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[f_J\,\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    \;+\; \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_J}\mathcal{M}_p\,
    \mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_J^c}\big]\;.
\end{equation}
Steps 2--4 bound the three terms of \eqref{eq:d2-split} in order.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Step 2: main term.}
Split the shell field into its tilted mean $\bar\varphi:=\langle\varphi_J(x)\rangle$ and centered part $\tilde\varphi:=\varphi_J(x)-\bar\varphi$.  Then $\mathcal{M}_p=\big\langle(B(x)+\bar\varphi+\tilde\varphi)^{2p}\big\rangle$ and $\langle\tilde\varphi\rangle=0$.  The centered moments obey
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-phit-mom}
    \big|\langle\tilde{\varphi}^{\,k}\rangle\big|
    \;\le\; (8c_q)^{k/2}\,\lceil k/2\rceil!\qquad(k\ge2)\;.
\end{equation}
For even $k$ this is~\eqref{eq:d2-cent}.  For odd $k=2m{+}1$ it follows from Cauchy--Schwarz between the orders $2m$ and $2m{+}2$, since $\sqrt{m!\,(m+1)!}\le(m+1)!$.

On $G_J$, the mean identity~\eqref{eq:mean-exact} bounds $|\bar\varphi|$ by $4t$ times the integral of $|\langle H_3(y)\rangle|$ against a measure $g(y)\,|\Gamma^\omega_J(x,y)|\,dy$ of total mass at most $\kappa 2^{-2J}$.  Jensen's inequality for that normalized measure moves the power $2p$ inside the integral.  For the integrand, \Cref{thm:d2-tilted} and~\eqref{eq:power-sum} give $|\langle H_3(y)\rangle|^{2p}\le\langle H_3(y)^2\rangle^p\le3^{p-1}C_H^p\big(1+B(y)^{6p}+D(y)^{3p}\big)$.  Under $\mu_{J-1}$, the inductive hypothesis bounds $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}[B(y)^{6p}]$ by $\mathcal{K}A_{3p}J^{3p}$, and $1+D(y)^{3p}\le2A_{3p}J^{3p}$ because $D\le c_qJ$ and $C_M\ge c_q\ge1$.  All together,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-mean-power}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[\,|\bar{\varphi}|^{2p}\,
    \mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    \;\le\; (4t\kappa)^{2p}\,2^{-4pJ}\,(3C_H)^p\,
    \mathcal{K}A_{3p}J^{3p}
    \;=:\;\mathcal{K}\,\Theta_p^{2p}.
\end{equation}
Two consequences follow: a bound on the size of the mean shift relative to that of the background, and the main-term estimate. First, using $(A_pJ^p)^{1/2p}\ge\sqrt{C_M Jp/e}$ and $\big((3p)!\big)^{1/2p}\le(3p)^{3/2}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-theta}
    \theta \;:=\;
    \frac{\Theta_p}{(A_pJ^p)^{1/2p}}
    \;\le\; C_\theta\,C_M\,p\,J\,2^{-2J}\;,
    \qquad C_\theta:=36\,t\,\kappa\sqrt{C_He}\;.
\end{equation}
Second, expand $(B(x)+\bar\varphi)^{2p}$ binomially and apply H\"older's inequality with exponents $\big(\tfrac{2p}{2p-k},\tfrac{2p}{k}\big)$ to the $k$-th term.  By~\eqref{eq:d2-mean-power} and the inductive hypothesis, the factors of $\mathcal{K}$ then recombine to first power:
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{E} \;:=\;
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[\big(B(x)+\bar\varphi\big)^{2p}\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    \;&\le\; \sum_{k=0}^{2p}\binom{2p}{k}
    \big(\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\big)^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}\,
    \big(\mathcal{K}\Theta_p^{2p}\big)^{\frac{k}{2p}}\notag\\[10 pt]
    &\;=\; \mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,(1+\theta)^{2p}
    \;\le\; \mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,e^{2p\theta}\label{eq:d2-T-bound}\;.
\end{align}
By the binomial theorem, $\langle\tilde\varphi\rangle=0$, \eqref{eq:d2-phit-mom}, and Jensen ($\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}[|B(x)+\bar\varphi|^{2p-k}\mathbf{1}_{G_J}]\le \mathcal{E}^{(2p-k)/2p}$),
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    \;\le\; \mathcal{E}+\sum_{k=2}^{2p}\binom{2p}{k}
    (8c_q)^{k/2}\,\lceil k/2\rceil!\;\mathcal{E}^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}.
\end{equation}
Each term is nondecreasing in $\mathcal{E}$ (its exponents are nonnegative), so $\mathcal{E}$ may be replaced throughout by its upper bound~\eqref{eq:d2-T-bound}, $\mathcal{E}_{\max}:=\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,e^{2p\theta}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-bracket}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    \;\le\; \mathcal{E}_{\max}\,\bigg(1+\sum_{k=2}^{2p}\binom{2p}{k}\,
    (8c_q)^{k/2}\,\lceil k/2\rceil!\;\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-k/2p}\bigg)\;.
\end{equation}
Now bound the summands.  Using $k!\ge\lceil k/2\rceil!\,\lfloor k/2\rfloor!$ in the first bound,
\begin{equation*}
    \binom{2p}{k}\lceil k/2\rceil!\;\le\;\frac{(2p)^k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor!}\;,
    \qquad
    \mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-k/2p}\;\le\;(A_pJ^p)^{-k/2p}\;\le\;\Big(\frac{e}{C_MJp}\Big)^{k/2}\;,
\end{equation*}
so the $k$-th summand is at most $\zeta^{\,k}/\lfloor k/2\rfloor!$ with $\zeta:=\big(32\,e\,c_q\,p/(C_M J)\big)^{1/2}$.  Splitting into even $k=2m$ and odd $k=2m{+}1$ (both with $\lfloor k/2\rfloor=m$) and extending the sums to infinity,
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{k=2}^{2p}\frac{\zeta^{\,k}}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor!}
    \;\le\; (1+\zeta)\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\zeta^{2m}}{m!}
    \;=\; (1+\zeta)\big(e^{\zeta^2}-1\big)
    \;\le\; e^{2\zeta^2}-1\;,
\end{equation*}
using $e^{2\zeta^2}-1=(e^{\zeta^2}-1)(e^{\zeta^2}+1)$ and $\zeta\le e^{\zeta^2}$ in the last step.  The bracket in~\eqref{eq:d2-bracket} is therefore at most $e^{2\zeta^2}$, and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-main-final}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    \;\le\; \mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,
    e^{2p\theta+2\zeta^2}\;,
    \qquad
    2\zeta^2 \;=\;\frac{u}{8}
    \quad\text{by } C_M=512\,e\,c_q\;,
\end{equation}
with $u:=p/J$.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Step 3: middle term.}
Insert the definition of $f_J$ into the middle term of~\eqref{eq:d2-split} and bound $D(y)^3\le(c_qJ)^3$. The term requiring work is $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}[B(y)^6\,\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}]$. H\"older with exponents $\big(\tfrac{p+3}{3},\tfrac{p+3}{p}\big)$, followed by Jensen's inequality under the tilted expectation, $\mathcal{M}_p^{(p+3)/p}\le\mathcal{M}_{p+3}$, gives two factors of order $p{+}3$:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[B(y)^6\,\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    \;\le\;
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[B(y)^{2(p+3)}\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]^{\frac{3}{p+3}}\;
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[\mathcal{M}_{p+3}\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]^{\frac{p}{p+3}}\;.
\end{equation*}
Bound the first factor by the level-$(J{-}1)$ hypothesis and the second by \eqref{eq:d2-main-final} at order $p{+}3$, writing $\theta',\zeta'$ for the order-$(p{+}3)$ instances of $\theta,\zeta$. The $\mathcal{K}$-powers, at exponents $\tfrac{3}{p+3}$ and $\tfrac{p}{p+3}$, recombine to first power:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[B(y)^6\,\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    &\;\le\;
    \big(\mathcal{K}A_{p+3}J^{p+3}\big)^{\frac{3}{p+3}}
    \Big(\mathcal{K}A_{p+3}J^{p+3}\,
    e^{2(p+3)\theta'+2\zeta'^2}\Big)^{\frac{p}{p+3}}\notag\\
    &\;\le\; \mathcal{K}A_{p+3}J^{p+3}\,
    e^{2(p+3)\theta'+2\zeta'^2}\;,\label{eq:step3-intermed}
\end{align}
dropping the exponent $\tfrac{p}{p+3}\le1$ from the exponential.  The other two pieces of $f_J$ give the main-term expectation $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}[\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}]$ times $1$ and $(c_qJ)^3$.  By~\eqref{eq:d2-main-final} and $(c_qJ)^3\,A_pJ^p\le C_M^3A_p\,J^{p+3} \le A_{p+3}J^{p+3}$, they take the same form as~\eqref{eq:step3-intermed} with exponential $e^{2p\theta+2\zeta^2}$.  Since $\zeta\le\zeta'$, all three pieces are bounded using the combined exponent $2p\theta+2(p{+}3)\theta'+2\zeta'^2$.  Integrating against $g$ and collecting constants into $C_2:=6\,C_\star\|g\|_1$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-small}
    2\,\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[f_J\,\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{G_J}\big]
    \;\le\; C_2\,2^{-2J}\,\mathcal{K}A_{p+3}\,
    J^{p+3}\,e^{2p\theta+2(p+3)\theta'+2\zeta'^2},
    \qquad
    2\zeta'^2 \;=\;\frac{p{+}3}{8J}\;.
\end{equation}

\medskip\noindent\textit{Step 4: complementary event.}
Since $e^{\Psi_J}\langle B_J(x)^{2p}\rangle=\mathbb{E}_{a_J}[e^{-X_J}B_J(x)^{2p}]$ and $Z_{J-1}\ge1$, the last term of \eqref{eq:d2-split} is at most an expectation under the free measure, with no factor of $\mathcal{K}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-large}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_J}\mathcal{M}_p\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_J^c}\big]
    \;\le\; \mathbb{E}_{\gamma_J}\big[e^{-V_J}\,B_J(x)^{2p}\,
    \mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_J^c}\big]
    \;\le\; e^{c_V(J+1)^2}
    \big(4p\,c_q(J{+}1)\big)^p\,
    \gamma_J\big(\mathcal{G}_J^c\big)^{1/2}
\end{equation}
by \Cref{lem:V-lower}, Cauchy--Schwarz, the Gaussian moments of Step~0, and the bound $(4p-1)!!\leq(4p)^{2p}$.  \Cref{cor:d2-bad-prob}, available because $J>J_\star\ge j_4$, bounds the probability of the bad event:
\begin{equation*}
    \gamma_J\big(\mathcal{G}_J^c\big)
    \;\le\; C_K\exp\!\Big(
    -\frac{c_3\,2^{2J/3}}{J}\Big)\;.
\end{equation*}

\medskip\noindent\textit{Step 5: closing.}
The three terms of~\eqref{eq:d2-split} are bounded by \eqref{eq:d2-main-final}, \eqref{eq:d2-small}, and~\eqref{eq:d2-large}, and the target is~\eqref{eq:d2-moments} at level $J$.  It suffices that the main term \eqref{eq:d2-main-final} be at most $\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,e^{u/4}$ and that \eqref{eq:d2-small} and \eqref{eq:d2-large} each be at most $\tfrac{u}{8}\,e^{u/4}\,\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p$.  Define $J_\star$ as the smallest integer at least $j_4$ such that for all $J>J_\star$ and all $p<p_1(J)$:
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:d2-Jstar}
\begin{align}
    16\,C_\theta\,C_M\,(p{+}3)^2J^2\,2^{-2J}
    &\;\le\; 1\;,
    \label{eq:d2-Jstar-a}\\[5 pt]
    C_3\,C_M^3\,(p{+}3)^3J^4\,2^{-2J}
    &\;\le\; 1\;,
    \label{eq:d2-Jstar-b}\\[5 pt]
    C_4J\,e^{c_V(J+1)^2+p/J}
    \exp\!\big(-c_3\,2^{2J/3}/(2J)\big)
    &\;\le\; 1\;,
    \label{eq:d2-Jstar-c}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
with $C_3:=8e^{1/2}C_2$ and $C_4:=8C_K^{1/2}$. Such a $J_\star$ exists.  Since $p<p_1(J)=O(J^2)$, the left sides of~\eqref{eq:d2-Jstar-a} and~\eqref{eq:d2-Jstar-b} are bounded by polynomials in $J$ times $2^{-2J}$, and in~\eqref{eq:d2-Jstar-c} the factor $e^{c_V(J+1)^2+p/J}=e^{O(J^2)}$ is outweighed by the super-exponential decay of $\exp\!\big(-c_3\,2^{2J/3}/(2J)\big)$.  All three left sides therefore tend to zero as $J\to\infty$, uniformly over $p<p_1(J)$.

For $J>J_\star$ and $p<p_1(J)$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item By~\eqref{eq:d2-Jstar-a} and~\eqref{eq:d2-theta}, $2p\theta\le1/(8J)\le u/8$, and with $2\zeta^2= u/8$ from~\eqref{eq:d2-main-final} the Step-2 term is at most $\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,e^{u/4}$.
\item The argument of~(a) at order $p+3$ gives $2(p+3)\theta'\leq 1/(8J)$.  With~(a) and $2\zeta'^2=(p+3)/(8J)$, the exponent of~\eqref{eq:d2-small} is at most $(p{+}5)/(8J)\le u/4+\tfrac12$.  The ratio of~\eqref{eq:d2-small} to $\tfrac{u}{8}e^{u/4}\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p$ is then at most the left side of~\eqref{eq:d2-Jstar-b}, so the Step-3 term is at most $\tfrac{u}{8}e^{u/4}\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p$.
\item Using $p^p\le e^p\,p!$ and $(1+1/J)^p\le e^{p/J}$, the ratio of~\eqref{eq:d2-large} to $\tfrac{u}{8}e^{u/4}\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p$ is at most the left side of~\eqref{eq:d2-Jstar-c} times $e^{-u/4}/(128^p\,p\,\mathcal{K})\le1$, so the Step-4 term is at most $\tfrac{u}{8}e^{u/4}\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p$.
\end{enumerate}
Collecting the three terms:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}\big[B_J(x)^{2p}\big]
    &\;\le\; \mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,e^{u/4}
    + \tfrac{u}{4}\,\mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,e^{u/4}
    \;=\; \mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,e^{u/4}\big(1+\tfrac{u}{4}\big)\notag\\[5 pt]
    &\;\le\; \mathcal{K}A_pJ^p\,e^{u/2}
    \;\le\; \mathcal{K}A_p(J{+}1)^p\;,
\end{align}
using $1+u/4\le e^{u/4}$ and then $u/2\le p\ln(1+1/J)$. Together with Step~0(i)--(ii), this establishes~\eqref{eq:d2-moments} for all $(J,p)$.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:d2-exp-int}
There is a constant $C_T>0$ such that for every $j\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-exp-int}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\big]
    \;\le\; 1 + C_T\,2^{-2j}\,j^3\;.
\end{equation}
The constant $C_T$ depends only on $t$, $g$, $c_q$, $\kappa$, and $j_4$.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
First suppose $j\geq j_4$. Since $D^\omega_{j-1}\le c_q\,j$ and $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}[B_{j-1}(y)^6]\le6\,\mathcal{K}C_M^3\,j^3$ at every $y\in K$ (\Cref{thm:d2-moments}),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-eta}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}[f_j]
    \;\le\; C_1\,2^{-2j}\,j^3\;,
    \qquad C_1\;:=\;C_\star\,\|g\|_1\big(1+6\,\mathcal{K}C_M^3+c_q^3\big)\;.
\end{equation}
On $\mathcal{G}_j$, $e^{\Psi_j}\le1+2f_j$, and hence $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}[e^{\Psi_j}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_j}]\le1+2C_1\,2^{-2j}j^3$.  Because $Z_{j-1}\ge1$, the expectation over $\mathcal{G}_j^c$ is at most $\mathbb{E}_{\gamma_j}[e^{-V_j}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_j^c}]$, and \Cref{lem:V-lower} and \Cref{cor:d2-bad-prob} bound this by $e^{c_V(j+1)^2}C_K\exp(-c_3\,2^{2j/3}/j)$, which is $o(2^{-2j}\,j^3)$.  Adding the two bounds gives~\eqref{eq:d2-exp-int} for $j\ge j_4$.  For the finitely many shells $1\le j<j_4$, equation \eqref{eq:telescope-step}, \Cref{lem:V-lower}, and~\eqref{eq:d2-C1} give $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}[e^{\Psi_j}]=Z_j/Z_{j-1}\le e^{c_V(j+1)^2}$, and enlarging $C_T$ covers these shells.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:d2-convergence}
The log partition function is given by
\begin{equation*}
    \log Z_\Lambda \;=\; \log Z_0
    +\sum_{j=1}^\Lambda\log\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\big]\;,
\end{equation*}
and for every $j\geq1$, the $j$-th summand satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-log-bound}
    0 \;\le\;
    \log\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\big]
    \;\le\; C_T\,2^{-2j}\,j^3\;.
\end{equation}
Since $\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-2j}\,j^3<\infty$, the sequence $\log Z_\Lambda$ converges as $\Lambda\to\infty$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
The displayed sum is \Cref{prop:telescope} after taking logarithms.  The lower bound in~\eqref{eq:d2-log-bound} follows from $\Psi_j\ge 0$ (\Cref{lem:positivity}).  The upper bound is \Cref{prop:d2-exp-int} together with $\log(1+u)\le u$ for $u\ge 0$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:d2-existence}
Fix a spatial cutoff $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^2;[0,\infty))$, not identically zero, with $K=\operatorname{supp} g$, and a width sequence $(N_j)_{j\ge 0}$ with $N_j\ge c_0\,(j{+}1)\,2^{4j}$ as in \eqref{eq:width-gen}. For $\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}$, let $Z_\Lambda$ and $\mu_\Lambda$ be the partition function and Gibbs measure of~\eqref{eq:Z-def}--\eqref{eq:mu-def} at shell cutoff $\Lambda$.  Then the following hold for almost every draw of the features:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item The partition function $Z_\Lambda$ converges to a limit $Z_\infty\in[1,\infty)$.

\item For integers $0\le L\le\Lambda$, let $\mu_{\Lambda}^{\le L}$ denote the marginal of $\mu_\Lambda$ on the shell coefficients $(a_0,\ldots,a_L)$.  For each $\Lambda'>\Lambda\geq L$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d_TV}
    d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mu_{\Lambda'}^{\le L},
    \,\mu_{\Lambda}^{\le L}\big)
    \;\le\; C_T\!\sum_{j=\Lambda+1}^{\Lambda'}
    2^{-2j}\,j^3\;,
\end{equation}
and in particular, for each $L$, these marginals are Cauchy in total variation as $\Lambda\to\infty$.  There is a unique probability measure $\mu_\infty$ on $\prod_{j\ge 0}\mathbb{R}^{N_j}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mu-infty}
    \mu_\infty^{\le L}
    \;=\;\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\mu_\Lambda^{\le L}
\end{equation}
in total variation for every $L$.

\item The field $\phi_\Lambda=\sum_{j=0}^\Lambda\varphi_j$ converges in distribution when smeared against test functions.  For any $f_1,\ldots,f_r\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^2)$ and any bounded continuous $F:\mathbb{R}^r\to\mathbb{R}$, as $\Lambda\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:joint-conv}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_\Lambda}\big[F\big(
    \langle\phi_\Lambda,f_1\rangle,\ldots,
    \langle\phi_\Lambda,f_r\rangle\big)\big]
    \;\longrightarrow\;
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}\big[F\big(
    \langle\phi_\infty,f_1\rangle,\ldots,
    \langle\phi_\infty,f_r\rangle\big)\big]\;,
\end{equation}
where the series $\langle\phi_\infty,f\rangle:=\sum_{j\ge0}\langle\varphi_j,f\rangle$ converges absolutely $\mu_\infty$-almost surely.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
\textit{Part (a).} By \Cref{thm:d2-convergence}, $Z_\Lambda$ converges to a finite limit $Z_\infty$, and $Z_\infty\ge1$ because $Z_\Lambda\ge1$ (\Cref{lem:V-lower}).

\medskip\noindent\textit{Part (b).}
For any bounded $\eta=\eta(a_0,\ldots,a_J)$, integrating out $a_{J+1}$ as in the proof of \Cref{prop:telescope} and dividing by $Z_{J+1}=Z_J\,\mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}[e^{\Psi_{J+1}}]$ gives
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{J+1}}[\eta]
    \;=\; \mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}\big[\eta\,\mathcal{R}_{J+1}\big]\;,
    \qquad
    \mathcal{R}_{J+1} \;:=\;
    \frac{e^{\Psi_{J+1}}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}[e^{\Psi_{J+1}}]}\;.
\end{equation*}
Hence $\mu_{J+1}^{\le J}$ has Radon--Nikodym derivative $\mathcal{R}_{J+1}$ with respect to $\mu_J$.  Since $\Psi_{J+1}\ge 0$ and $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}[e^{\Psi_{J+1}}]\ge 1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:RN_identity}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}\big|\mathcal{R}_{J+1}-1\big|
    \;\le\; 2\big(\mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}[e^{\Psi_{J+1}}]-1\big)\;.
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:dTV-L1}, \eqref{eq:RN_identity}, and \eqref{eq:d2-exp-int}:
\begin{equation*}
    d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu_{J+1}^{\le J},\mu_J) =\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_J}|\mathcal{R}_{J+1}{-}1| \le C_T\,2^{-2(J+1)}(J{+}1)^3\;.
\end{equation*}
For $L\leq J$, marginalizing to the first $L+1$ shells does not increase total variation distance because an event in those shells is also an event in the first $J+1$ shells.  The same bound therefore holds for $d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mu_{J+1}^{\le L},\mu_J^{\le L}\big)$, and summing over $J=\Lambda,\ldots,\Lambda'-1$ with the triangle inequality gives~\eqref{eq:d_TV}. The right side of \eqref{eq:d_TV} is at most $C_T$ times the tail $\sum_{j>\Lambda}2^{-2j}j^3$, which vanishes as $\Lambda\to\infty$, so the marginals are Cauchy in total variation.

Fix $L$ and represent each marginal by its density against the free measure $\gamma_L$.  Integrating out the shells above $L$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:marg-density}
    \frac{d\mu_\Lambda^{\le L}}{d\gamma_L}
    \;=\;\frac{1}{Z_\Lambda}\int e^{-V_\Lambda}\,
    d\gamma_\sigma(a_{L+1})\cdots d\gamma_\sigma(a_\Lambda)\;.
\end{equation}
By Scheff\'e's theorem,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:marg-L1}
    d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mu_{\Lambda'}^{\le L},\mu_{\Lambda}^{\le L}\big)
    \;=\;\frac{1}{2}
    \bigg\|\frac{d\mu_{\Lambda'}^{\le L}}{d\gamma_L}
    -\frac{d\mu_{\Lambda}^{\le L}}{d\gamma_L}
    \bigg\|_{L^1(\gamma_L)}\;.
\end{equation}
Since the marginals are Cauchy in total variation, the densities are Cauchy in $L^1(\gamma_L)$, and by completeness \cite[Lem.~1.33]{Kallenberg2021} they converge in $L^1(\gamma_L)$.  Let $\hat\mu_L$ be the measure whose density against $\gamma_L$ is their limit.  Because nonnegativity and integration to one pass to $L^1$ limits, $\hat\mu_L$ is a probability measure.  Scheff\'e's theorem applied to $\mu_\Lambda^{\le L}$ and $\hat\mu_L$ then gives $\mu_\Lambda^{\le L}\to\hat\mu_L$ in total variation.

Fix $L'\ge L$.  At every cutoff, marginalizing $\mu_\Lambda^{\le L'}$ onto the first $L+1$ shells returns $\mu_\Lambda^{\le L}$, and marginalizing does not increase total variation distance, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:marg-consist}
    d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mu_\Lambda^{\le L},\,(\hat\mu_{L'})^{\le L}\big)
    \;\le\; d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mu_\Lambda^{\le L'},\,\hat\mu_{L'}\big)
    \;\xrightarrow[\Lambda\to\infty]{}\;0\;.
\end{equation}
The marginals $\mu_\Lambda^{\le L}$ therefore converge to both $\hat\mu_L$ and $(\hat\mu_{L'})^{\le L}$.  Limits in total variation are unique, so $(\hat\mu_{L'})^{\le L}=\hat\mu_L$, and the family $(\hat\mu_L)_{L\ge0}$ is consistent.

Finally, by Daniell's theorem \cite[Thm.~8.21]{Kallenberg2021}, there is a probability measure $\mu_\infty$ on $\prod_{j\ge0}\mathbb{R}^{N_j}$ with $\mu_\infty^{\le L}=\hat\mu_L$ for every $L$, and these marginals determine $\mu_\infty$ uniquely \cite[Lem.~1.18]{Kallenberg2021}.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Part (c).}
Fix $f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^2)$, write $w_j:=\langle\varphi_j,f\rangle$ for each shell, and let $\mathcal{X}_\Lambda(s):=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\Lambda}[e^{is\langle\phi_\Lambda,f\rangle}]$ for $s\in\mathbb{R}$.  For bounded $\eta=\eta(a_0,\ldots,a_\Lambda)$, Scheff\'e's theorem and~\eqref{eq:d_TV} with $L=\Lambda$ and $\Lambda'\to\infty$ give
\begin{align}
    \big|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\Lambda}[\eta]-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}[\eta]\big|
    &\;\le\;\sup|\eta|\,\bigg\|\frac{d\mu_\Lambda}{d\gamma_\Lambda}-\frac{d\mu_\infty^{\le\Lambda}}{d\gamma_\Lambda}\bigg\|_{L^1(\gamma_\Lambda)}\notag\\[5 pt]
    &\;=\;2\sup|\eta|\,d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mu_\infty^{\le\Lambda},\mu_\Lambda\big)
    \;\le\;2C_T\sup|\eta|\sum_{j>\Lambda}2^{-2j}j^3\;.\label{eq:TV-tail}
\end{align}
As in~\eqref{eq:d2-ratio}, and because $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}[e^{\Psi_j}]\ge1$, every bounded $\eta=\eta(a_0,\ldots,a_j)\ge0$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:top-shell}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_j}[\eta]\;\le\;\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\langle \eta\rangle\big]\;,
\end{equation}
where $\langle\cdot\rangle$ is the tilted expectation over $a_j$.

Fix $j>j_4$, so that \Cref{cor:BL}, \Cref{thm:d2-tilted}, and \Cref{cor:d2-bad-prob} apply at shell $j$.  First, set $v_\ell:=\langle U_{j,\ell},f\rangle$, so that $w_j=v^Ta_j$.  Each $U_{j,\ell}$ is a cosine of frequency $|b_{j,\ell}|\ge2^{j-1}$ times the amplitude $(2q_j/(N_j\sigma^2))^{1/2}$, and the Laplacian acting on the cosine multiplies it by $-|b_{j,\ell}|^2$.  Moving the Laplacian onto $f$ by two integrations by parts, which leave no boundary terms because $f$ is a Schwartz function, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-fourier}
    |v_\ell| \;\le\; \frac{C_f}{2^{2j}\sqrt{N_j}}\,,\qquad C_f:=4\sqrt{\frac{2q_{\max}}{\sigma^2}}\,\|\Delta f\|_{L^1}\;.
\end{equation}
Squaring and summing over the $N_j$ features gives $|v|\le C_f2^{-2j}$.

Second, $w_j$ is a linear functional of $a_j$, and by \Cref{cor:BL} the log-density has Hessian at least $\tfrac{1}{2\sigma^2}I_{N_j}$.  The Brascamp--Lieb inequality \cite[Thm.~4.1]{BrascampLieb1976} therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:var-w}
    \operatorname{Var}(w_j)\;\le\;2\sigma^2|v|^2\;\le\;2\sigma^2C_f^2\,2^{-4j}\;.
\end{equation}

Third, the mean identity~\eqref{eq:mean-exact}, together with $\Gamma^\omega_j(x,y)=\sigma^2\sum_\ell U_{j,\ell}(x)U_{j,\ell}(y)$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-w-mean}
    \langle w_j\rangle
    \;=\;\int f\,\bar{\varphi}_j\,dx
    \;=\; -4t\int g(y)\,\Big(\sigma^2\textstyle\sum_\ell
    v_\ell\,U_{j,\ell}(y)\Big)\,
    \Big\langle H_3\big(B_j(y); D^\omega_j(y)\big)\Big\rangle\,dy\;.
\end{equation}
Cauchy--Schwarz over $\ell$ bounds the parentheses,
\begin{equation*}
    \sigma^2\Big|\sum_\ell v_\ell\,U_{j,\ell}(y)\Big|
    \;\le\;\sigma|v|\sqrt{q^\omega_j(y)}
    \;\le\;\sigma\sqrt{c_q}\,C_f\,2^{-2j}\;.
\end{equation*}
The $\langle H_3\rangle$ factor in the integrand of~\eqref{eq:d2-w-mean} is at most $\langle H_3^2\rangle^{1/2}$ in absolute value, which \Cref{thm:d2-tilted} bounds on $G_j$.

Splitting $w_j$ into its tilted mean and fluctuation gives $\langle|w_j|\rangle\le|\langle w_j\rangle|+\operatorname{Var}(w_j)^{1/2}$.  Averaging under $\mu_{j-1}$, using $e^{\Psi_j}\le e$ on $\mathcal{G}_j$, Jensen's inequality for the square root, and the sixth moment bound $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}[B_{j-1}(y)^6]\le6\,\mathcal{K}C_M^3j^3$ (\Cref{thm:d2-moments}), gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-good-event}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\langle|w_j|\rangle\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_j}\big]
    \;\le\;C_f'\,2^{-2j}j^{3/2}\;.
\end{equation}
The proof of \Cref{prop:d2-exp-int} also gives $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}[e^{\Psi_j}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_j^c}]\le C_K\,e^{c_V(j+1)^2}\exp(-c_3\,2^{2j/3}/j)$.

Taking $\eta=\min\{|w_j|,1\}$ in~\eqref{eq:top-shell} and bounding $\langle\eta\rangle$ by $\langle|w_j|\rangle$ on $\mathcal{G}_j$ and by $1$ on $\mathcal G_j^c$ gives
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_j}\big[\min\{|w_j|,1\}\big]
    &\;\le\;\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\langle|w_j|\rangle\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_j}\big]
    +\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{j-1}}\big[e^{\Psi_j}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_j^c}\big]\\
    &\;\le\;C_f'\,2^{-2j}j^{3/2}+C_K\,e^{c_V(j+1)^2}\exp\!\big(-c_3\,2^{2j/3}/j\big)\;,
\end{align*}
and replacing $\mu_j$ by $\mu_\infty$ adds at most $2C_T\sum_{k>j}2^{-2k}k^3$, by~\eqref{eq:TV-tail} at $\Lambda=j$.  All three terms are $O(2^{-2j}j^3)$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d2-w-first}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}\big[\min\{|w_j|,1\}\big]\;\le\;C_1\,2^{-2j}j^3\qquad(j>j_4)\;,
\end{equation}
where $C_1$ is built from $C_f'$, $C_K$, $c_V$, $c_3$, and $C_T$.

By monotone convergence and~\eqref{eq:d2-w-first}, $\sum_j\min\{|w_j|,1\}$ has finite expectation under $\mu_\infty$.  Almost surely, therefore, $|w_j|<1$ for all large $j$, and the shell series $\langle\phi_\infty,f\rangle=\sum_jw_j$ converges absolutely.

Fix $s\in\mathbb{R}$ and $\Lambda\ge j_4$, and insert $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}[e^{is\langle\phi_\Lambda,f\rangle}]$ between $\mathcal{X}_\Lambda(s)$ and $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}[e^{is\langle\phi_\infty,f\rangle}]$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:char-split}
    \Big|\mathcal{X}_\Lambda(s)-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}\big[e^{is\langle\phi_\infty,f\rangle}\big]\Big|
    \le\Big|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\Lambda}\big[e^{is\langle\phi_\Lambda,f\rangle}\big]-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}\big[e^{is\langle\phi_\Lambda,f\rangle}\big]\Big|
    +\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}\Big|e^{is\langle\phi_\Lambda,f\rangle}-e^{is\langle\phi_\infty,f\rangle}\Big|\;.
\end{equation}
The exponential $e^{is\langle\phi_\Lambda,f\rangle}$ has modulus one and depends only on the first $\Lambda{+}1$ shells, so~\eqref{eq:TV-tail} bounds the first term by $2C_T\sum_{j>\Lambda}2^{-2j}j^3$.  For the second term, pointwise,
\begin{equation*}
