    \big|e^{is\langle\phi_\Lambda,f\rangle}-e^{is\langle\phi_\infty,f\rangle}\big|
    \;\le\;(2+|s|)\sum_{j>\Lambda}\min\{|w_j|,1\}\;.
\end{equation*}
Indeed, the left side is at most $2$ and at most $|s|\,|\langle\phi_\infty,f\rangle-\langle\phi_\Lambda,f\rangle|\le|s|\sum_{j>\Lambda}|w_j|$, while the right side is at least $2$ if some $|w_j|$ exceeds $1$, and at least $|s|\sum_{j>\Lambda}|w_j|$ otherwise.  By~\eqref{eq:d2-w-first}, the second term of \eqref{eq:char-split} is therefore at most $(2+|s|)\,C_1\sum_{j>\Lambda}2^{-2j}j^3$.  Adding the two bounds,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:char-rate}
    \Big|\mathcal{X}_\Lambda(s)-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}\big[e^{is\langle\phi_\infty,f\rangle}\big]\Big|
    \;\le\;C_{\mathcal{X}}\,(1+|s|)\sum_{j>\Lambda}2^{-2j}j^3\;,
\end{equation}
with $C_{\mathcal{X}}:=2C_T+2C_1$.

Given $f_1,\ldots,f_r\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^2)$ and $s\in\mathbb{R}^r$, the function $h:=\sum_ks_kf_k$ is again Schwartz, and~\eqref{eq:char-rate} for $h$ at the point $1$ gives $\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\Lambda}[e^{i\langle\phi_\Lambda,h\rangle}]\to\mathbb{E}_{\mu_\infty}[e^{i\langle\phi_\infty,h\rangle}]$.  Since the pairing is linear and the shell series converge absolutely, $\langle\phi_\Lambda,h\rangle=\sum_ks_k\langle\phi_\Lambda,f_k\rangle$, and almost surely $\langle\phi_\infty,h\rangle=\sum_ks_k\langle\phi_\infty,f_k\rangle$.  The characteristic functions of $(\langle\phi_\Lambda,f_k\rangle)_{k\le r}$ under $\mu_\Lambda$ therefore converge pointwise to that of $(\langle\phi_\infty,f_k\rangle)_{k\le r}$ under $\mu_\infty$, and the L\'evy continuity theorem \cite[Thm.~6.23]{Kallenberg2021} gives~\eqref{eq:joint-conv}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:CK-conv}
For each fixed $\Lambda$, the laws $P^{0,(n)}_\Lambda$ are tight in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, and almost surely in $\omega^\infty$ they converge weakly to $P^{0,(\infty)}_\Lambda$ as $n\to\infty$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Under $\gamma_\Lambda^{(n)}$ the field $\phi_\Lambda^{(n)}$ is a centered Gaussian process on $\mathbb{R}^d$, continuous because it is a finite sum of cosines.  Its pointwise variance is $D^{\omega^{(n)}}_\Lambda(x)\le2D_\Lambda$, and by \Cref{lem:incr-var} its increments have variance at most $\Upsilon_\Lambda|x-y|^2$.  Neither bound involves $n$.  Since the increments are Gaussian, the second bound controls every moment:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CK-moments}
    \sup_n\,\mathbb{E}_{\gamma^{(n)}_\Lambda}\big|\phi_\Lambda^{(n)}(x)-\phi_\Lambda^{(n)}(y)\big|^{2p}
    \;\le\;(2p-1)!!\,\Upsilon_\Lambda^{p}\,|x-y|^{2p},
    \qquad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d,\quad p\ge1\;.
\end{equation}
Fix $p$ with $2p>d$.  The values at a single point are tight, because their variances are at most $2D_\Lambda$, and~\eqref{eq:CK-moments} is the moment condition of \cite[Thm.~23.7]{Kallenberg2021} with exponents $a=2p$ and $b=2p-d$.  By that theorem, the family $\{P^{0,(n)}_\Lambda\}_n$ is tight in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)$.

It remains to identify the limit, first for the finite-dimensional distributions.  Fix $x_1,\dots,x_r\in\mathbb{R}^d$.  The vector $(\phi_\Lambda^{(n)}(x_i))_{i\le r}$ is centered Gaussian with covariance $\sum_{j\le\Lambda}\Gamma_j^{\omega^{(n)}}(x_i,x_{i'})$, which converges to $Q_{\le\Lambda}(x_i-x_{i'})$ by \Cref{prop:kernel-conv}.  The characteristic function of a centered Gaussian vector depends continuously on its covariance, so the L\'evy continuity theorem \cite[Thm.~6.23]{Kallenberg2021} gives convergence of the finite-dimensional distributions of $P^{0,(n)}_\Lambda$ to those of $P^{0,(\infty)}_\Lambda$.

Together with tightness, convergence of the finite-dimensional distributions gives $P^{0,(n)}_\Lambda\to P^{0,(\infty)}_\Lambda$ weakly in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ \cite[Thm.~23.4 and Cor.~23.5]{Kallenberg2021}.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:cont-conv}
Let $S$ be a metric space, let $P_n$ converge weakly to $P$ on $S$, and let $h_n,\,h:S\to\mathbb{R}$ satisfy $\sup_n\|h_n\|_\infty<\infty$, $h$ continuous, and $h_n(x_n)\to h(x)$ whenever $x_n\to x$ in $S$.  Then $\int h_n\,dP_n\to\int h\,dP$.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Let $X_n\sim P_n$ and $X\sim P$, so that $\int h_n\,dP_n=\mathbb{E}[h_n(X_n)]$ and $\int h\,dP=\mathbb{E}[h(X)]$.  The hypothesis on the $h_n$ is that of the continuous mapping theorem \cite[Thm.~5.27]{Kallenberg2021}, which gives $h_n(X_n)\to h(X)$ in distribution.  These variables are bounded by $\sup_n\|h_n\|_\infty$, which also bounds $h$, and for uniformly bounded variables convergence in distribution gives convergence of expectations.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:infwidth}
Almost surely in $\omega^\infty$, for each fixed $\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}$ the laws $P^{(n)}_\Lambda$ converge weakly in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ as $n\to\infty$ to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mu-std-def}
    dP^{(\infty)}_\Lambda
    \;:=\;\frac{e^{-V(\phi;\,D_\Lambda)}}
    {Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda}\,dP^{0,(\infty)}_\Lambda\;,
    \qquad
    Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda
    \;:=\;\int e^{-V(\phi;\,D_\Lambda)}\,
    dP^{0,(\infty)}_\Lambda\;,
\end{equation}
the Gibbs measure of the truncated free field.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Let $G:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)\to\mathbb{R}$ be bounded and continuous, and set $\tilde h_n(\phi):=e^{-V(\phi;\,D^{\omega^{(n)}}_\Lambda)}$, so that the Gibbs density in~\eqref{eq:mu-def} is $e^{-V^{(n)}_\Lambda}/Z^{(n)}_\Lambda=\big(\tilde h_n\circ\iota^{(n)}_\Lambda\big)/Z^{(n)}_\Lambda$.  The substitution rule \cite[Lem.~1.24]{Kallenberg2021} gives the first and last equalities below,
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E}_{P^{(n)}_\Lambda}[G]
    &\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(n)}_\Lambda}\big[G\circ\iota^{(n)}_\Lambda\big]
    \;=\;\frac{1}{Z^{(n)}_\Lambda}\,\mathbb{E}_{\gamma^{(n)}_\Lambda}\big[(G\,\tilde h_n)\circ\iota^{(n)}_\Lambda\big]\\
    &\;=\;\frac{1}{Z^{(n)}_\Lambda}\int G\,\tilde h_n\,dP^{0,(n)}_\Lambda\;.
\end{align*}
That is, $P^{(n)}_\Lambda$ has density $\tilde h_n/Z^{(n)}_\Lambda$ against $P^{0,(n)}_\Lambda$, the finite-width form of~\eqref{eq:mu-std-def}.  At $G\equiv1$ this reads $Z^{(n)}_\Lambda=\int\tilde h_n\,dP^{0,(n)}_\Lambda$, so with $h_n:=G\,\tilde h_n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ratio-form}
    \mathbb{E}_{P^{(n)}_\Lambda}[G]
    \;=\;\frac{\int h_n\,dP^{0,(n)}_\Lambda}
    {\int\tilde h_n\,dP^{0,(n)}_\Lambda}\;.
\end{equation}
We verify the hypotheses of \Cref{lem:cont-conv} for both integrands, with $S=\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, $P_n=P^{0,(n)}_\Lambda$, and $P=P^{0,(\infty)}_\Lambda$, which converge weakly by \Cref{lem:CK-conv}.

\textit{Uniform boundedness.}  By~\eqref{eq:H4-lower}, $V(\phi;D^{\omega^{(n)}}_\Lambda)\ge-6t\|g\|_1\|D^{\omega^{(n)}}_\Lambda\|_\infty^2$ for every $\phi\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, and $\|D^{\omega^{(n)}}_\Lambda\|_\infty\le2D_\Lambda$ because $q^\omega_j\le2q_j$.  Hence, for every $n$,
\begin{equation*}
    \|h_n\|_\infty \;\le\; \|G\|_\infty\,
    \exp\big(24t\|g\|_1D_\Lambda^2\big)\;.
\end{equation*}

\textit{Continuous convergence.}  Let $\phi_n\to\phi$ in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, so that $\phi_n\to\phi$ uniformly on $K$.  By \Cref{prop:kernel-conv}, the Wick constants $D^{\omega^{(n)}}_\Lambda$ also converge to $D_\Lambda$ uniformly on $K$.  For $x\in K$, the pairs $(\phi_n(x),D^{\omega^{(n)}}_\Lambda(x))$ stay in a fixed compact set, and $(u,D)\mapsto H_4(u;D)$ is uniformly continuous there.  Hence $H_4(\phi_n;D^{\omega^{(n)}}_\Lambda)\to H_4(\phi;D_\Lambda)$ uniformly on $K$, and integrating against the finite measure $g\,dx$ gives $V(\phi_n;D^{\omega^{(n)}}_\Lambda)\to V(\phi;D_\Lambda)$.  Since $G$ is continuous, it follows that $h_n(\phi_n)\to h(\phi)$ and $\tilde h_n(\phi_n)\to\tilde h(\phi)$, where
\begin{equation*}
    h(\phi):=G(\phi)\,e^{-V(\phi;\,D_\Lambda)}\;,
    \qquad
    \tilde h(\phi):=e^{-V(\phi;\,D_\Lambda)}
\end{equation*}
are continuous on $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)$.

\Cref{lem:cont-conv} thus applies to the numerator and the denominator of~\eqref{eq:ratio-form}.  The denominator converges to $Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$, which is at least $1$ because every $Z^{(n)}_\Lambda$ is (\Cref{lem:V-lower}). The ratio therefore converges, and
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}_{P^{(n)}_\Lambda}[G]\;\to\;\mathbb{E}_{P^{(\infty)}_\Lambda}[G]\;.
\end{equation}
Since $G$ was an arbitrary bounded continuous functional on $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, the laws $P^{(n)}_\Lambda$ converge weakly to $P^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{prop:uniform-N}
Work on the almost-sure event of \Cref{lem:unif-gg}.  Then \Cref{sec:d2-setup,sec:d2-converge} apply verbatim to every network of \Cref{def:nested}, with $j_0$ read as $j_0^*$, and their constants and thresholds do not depend on $n$.  In particular, the constant $C_{\mathcal{X}}$ of~\eqref{eq:char-rate} is free of $n$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
The features enter the estimates of \Cref{sec:one-shell,sec:d2-setup,sec:d2-converge} in only two ways.  The first is through the bounds of \Cref{thm:good-geom} above the threshold $j_0$, and \Cref{lem:unif-gg} gives these for every $n$ with the same threshold $j_0^*$ and the same constant $\kappa$.  The second is through bounds that hold for every realization, namely $q^\omega_j\le2q_j$ and the fact that every frequency of shell $j\ge1$ lies in the annulus $2^{j-1}\le|p|\le2^{j+1}$.  Every other constant and threshold in these sections is built from these bounds, from $j_0^*$, and from the fixed data, together with $\|\Delta f\|_{L^1}$ for the constant of~\eqref{eq:char-rate}.  None of them depends on $n$.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:width-smeared}
Almost surely in $\omega^\infty$, for every $\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}$, every $r$, and all $f_1,\dots,f_r\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^2)$, the law of $\big(\langle\phi_\Lambda^{(n)},f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $\mu^{(n)}_\Lambda$ converges weakly on $\mathbb{R}^r$, as $n\to\infty$, to the law of $\big(\langle\phi,f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $P^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Work on the probability-one event of \Cref{thm:infwidth}, fix $\Lambda$ and $s\in\mathbb{R}^r$, and set $h:=\sum_ks_kf_k$ and $h_R:=h\,\mathbf{1}_{\{|x|\le R\}}$.  Because convergence in $\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^2)$ is uniform on compact sets, the map $\phi\mapsto e^{i\langle\phi,h_R\rangle}$ is bounded and continuous.  \Cref{thm:infwidth} therefore gives convergence of the characteristic functions at $s$ with $h_R$ in place of $h$.  By \Cref{lem:V-lower}, Cauchy--Schwarz under the Gaussian prior, and $q^\omega_j\le2q_j$, the first moments $\mathbb{E}|\phi(x)|$ are bounded by a constant free of $x$ and $n$, under every $\mu^{(n)}_\Lambda$ and under $P^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$.  Since $|e^{ia}-e^{ib}|\le|a-b|$, replacing $h$ by $h_R$ therefore changes each characteristic function by at most a constant times $\int_{|x|>R}|h|\,dx$, uniformly in $n$.  Letting $n\to\infty$ and then $R\to\infty$ gives convergence of the characteristic functions at $s$, and the L\'evy continuity theorem \cite[Thm.~6.23]{Kallenberg2021} gives the claimed weak convergence.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:interchange}
Almost surely in $\omega^\infty$, for every $r$ and all $f_1,\dots,f_r\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^2)$, the double sequence
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\Lambda(s)
    \;:=\;\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(n)}_\Lambda}
    \Big[\exp\Big(i\textstyle\sum_k s_k
    \langle\phi_\Lambda^{(n)},f_k\rangle\Big)\Big],
    \qquad s\in\mathbb{R}^r\;,
\end{equation*}
has both iterated limits, and they agree:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:interchange}
    \lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\Lambda(s)
    \;=\;\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\Lambda(s)
    \;=:\;\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty(s)\;.
\end{equation}
The common value is continuous on $\mathbb{R}^r$ with $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty(0)=1$.  Along either order of limits the law of the smeared vector $\big(\langle\phi_\Lambda^{(n)},f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $\mu^{(n)}_\Lambda$ therefore converges weakly on $\mathbb{R}^r$ to the law with characteristic function $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Work on the intersection of the probability-one events of \Cref{lem:unif-gg} and \Cref{thm:infwidth}, and fix $r$ and $f_1,\dots,f_r$. At each cutoff, \Cref{lem:width-smeared} gives the width limit $\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\Lambda\to\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$, where $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$ is the characteristic function of $(\langle\phi,f_k\rangle)_{k\le r}$ under $P^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$.  For the cutoff limit at each width, write $\mu^{(n)}_\infty$ for the measure $\mu_\infty$ of \Cref{thm:d2-existence} at the widths~\eqref{eq:width-schedule}, and $\langle\phi_\infty^{(n)},f\rangle$ for the corresponding series $\langle\phi_\infty,f\rangle$.  Part~(c) of that theorem gives $\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\Lambda\to\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\infty$ as $\Lambda\to\infty$, where $\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\infty$ is the characteristic function of $(\langle\phi_\infty^{(n)},f_k\rangle)_{k\le r}$ under $\mu^{(n)}_\infty$.  The cutoff limit is uniform in the width.  Indeed, $\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\Lambda(s)$ is the characteristic function of $\langle\phi_\Lambda^{(n)},h\rangle$ at $1$, with $h:=\sum_ks_kf_k$, so~\eqref{eq:char-rate} applied to $h$ gives, for $\Lambda\ge j_4$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unif-width}
    \big|\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\Lambda(s)-\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\infty(s)\big|
    \;\le\; 2C_{\mathcal{X}}\sum_{j>\Lambda}2^{-2j}j^3\;,
\end{equation}
in which $C_{\mathcal{X}}$, computed for $h$, does not depend on $n$ (\Cref{prop:uniform-N}) and is bounded for $s$ in compact subsets of $\mathbb{R}^r$.

The Moore--Osgood theorem \cite[Thm.~7.11]{Rudin1976} therefore applies, with $\Lambda$ as its sequence index and $1/n$ ranging over $\{1/n:n\ge1\}$, which has $0$ as a limit point.  Its uniformity hypothesis is~\eqref{eq:unif-width}, and its limit hypothesis is the width limit $\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\Lambda\to\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$.  The theorem then yields~\eqref{eq:interchange}, with common value $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty(s)=\lim_\Lambda\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\Lambda(s)=\lim_n\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\infty(s)$.

Letting $n\to\infty$ in~\eqref{eq:unif-width} preserves the bound, so $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\Lambda\to\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty$ uniformly on compact subsets of $\mathbb{R}^r$.  Each $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$ is a characteristic function and hence continuous, so the limit is continuous. Furthermore, $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty(0)=1$ because $\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\Lambda(0)=1$ for every $n$ and $\Lambda$.  The L\'evy continuity theorem \cite[Thm.~6.23]{Kallenberg2021}, applied to $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\Lambda\to\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty$ and to $\mathcal{X}^{(n)}_\infty\to\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty$, gives in each case weak convergence on $\mathbb{R}^r$ to the law with characteristic function $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty$.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:LP-standard}
Let $d=2$ and $t>0$, and write $V(\,\cdot\,;\,\cdot\,)$ for the field-space interaction~\eqref{eq:V-field}.  Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $Q\in L^4(\mathbb{R}^2)$ and $\|Q-Q_{\le\Lambda}\|_{L^4(\mathbb{R}^2)}\le c\,2^{-\Lambda/2}$.  Consequently the interaction $V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)$ converges in $L^2(d\upphi_Q)$, and we write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wick-limit}
    V\big(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda\big)
    \;\xrightarrow[\Lambda\to\infty]{L^2(d\upphi_Q)}\;
    V\;=:\;t\int g(x):\!\upphi(x)^4\!:_Q dx\;.
\end{equation}
The right side is the smeared Wick power, defined by this limit, since $:\!\upphi(x)^4\!:_Q$ exists only after integration against a test function. The same limit arises from mollifying the field, as in \cite[Prop.~8.5.1]{GlimmJaffe1987}, and $V$ is a function of $\upphi$.
\item Let $\nu$ be the finite-volume Wick-ordered $\phi^4_2$ measure built from the full propagator,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:phi42-standard}
    d\nu\;=\;\frac{e^{-V}}{\int e^{-V}d\upphi_Q}\,d\upphi_Q\;.
\end{equation}
For all $f_1,\dots,f_r\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^2)$ the law of $\big(\langle\upphi_{\le\Lambda},f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $P^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$ converges weakly on $\mathbb{R}^r$, as $\Lambda\to\infty$, to the law of $\big(\langle\upphi,f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $\nu$.  Consequently, almost surely in $\omega^\infty$, the common value $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty$ of~\eqref{eq:interchange} is the characteristic function of $\big(\langle\upphi,f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $\nu$, and the law of $\big(\langle\phi_\infty^{(n)},f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $\mu^{(n)}_\infty$ converges weakly to the same law as $n\to\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
\textit{Part (a).}  By \Cref{lem:shell-dim}(a)--(c) at $d=2$, the shell kernels obey $\|Q_j\|_{L^\infty}= q_j\le C$ and $\|Q_j\|_{L^1}\le C2^{-2j}$, so
\begin{equation}
    \|Q_j\|_{L^4}^4 \;\le\; \|Q_j\|_{L^\infty}^3\,\|Q_j\|_{L^1}\leq C^42^{-2j}\;,
    \qquad\text{whence}\qquad
    \|Q_j\|_{L^4} \;\le\; C\,2^{-j/2}\;.
\end{equation}
Summing the tail gives $\|Q-Q_{\le\Lambda}\|_{L^4}\le\sum_{j>\Lambda}\|Q_j\|_{L^4}\le c\,2^{-\Lambda/2}$. At $\Lambda=0$ this bound covers $\sum_{j\ge1}Q_j$, and $Q_0$, the Fourier transform of the smooth, compactly supported $S_0$, is a Schwartz function, so $Q\in L^4$.

Because the $\upphi^{(j)}$ in~\eqref{eq:phi-shells} are independent, for $\Lambda\le\Lambda'$ those above $\Lambda$ contribute nothing to the cross-covariance,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nesting}
    \operatorname{Cov}\big(\upphi_{\le\Lambda}(x),\upphi_{\le\Lambda'}(y)\big)
    \;=\; Q_{\le\Lambda}(x-y)\;,
\end{equation}
and the covariance at coincident points gives $\operatorname{Var}\upphi_{\le\Lambda}(x)=Q_{\le\Lambda}(0)=D_\Lambda$.  For centered jointly Gaussian $X$ and $Y$, Hermite orthogonality \cite[Thm.~3.9]{Janson1997} gives
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\big[H_4(X;\operatorname{Var} X)\,H_4(Y;\operatorname{Var} Y)\big]\;=\;4!\,\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)^4\;.
\end{equation*}
Expanding the square below gives two diagonal terms and a cross term, each a double integral against $g(x)g(y)$ to which this identity applies. The diagonal terms contribute $Q_{\le\Lambda'}^4(x-y)$ and $Q_{\le\Lambda}^4(x-y)$, while the cross term's covariance is set by the smaller truncation by \eqref{eq:nesting}.  Hence,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wick-cauchy}
    \mathbb{E}_{\upphi_Q}\Big[\big(V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda'};D_{\Lambda'})
    -V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)\big)^{2}\Big]
    \;=\; 4!\,t^2\!\iint g(x)g(y)
    \big[Q_{\le\Lambda'}^4-Q_{\le\Lambda}^4\big](x-y)\,dx\,dy\;.
\end{equation}
It remains to bound the right side of~\eqref{eq:wick-cauchy}.  Since $\iint g(x)g(y)|h(x-y)|\,dx\,dy\le\|g\|_{L^\infty}\|g\|_{L^1}\|h\|_{L^1}$, it is enough to bound the kernel difference in $L^1$.  From $|a^4-b^4|\leq|a-b|(|a|+|b|)^3$ and H\"older with exponents $4$ and $4/3$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fourth-power}
    \big\|Q_{\le\Lambda'}^4-Q_{\le\Lambda}^4\big\|_{L^1}
    \;\le\; \big\|Q_{\le\Lambda'}-Q_{\le\Lambda}\big\|_{L^4}
    \big(\|Q_{\le\Lambda'}\|_{L^4}+\|Q_{\le\Lambda}\|_{L^4}\big)^3\;.
\end{equation}
The first factor is at most $\sum_{\Lambda<j\le\Lambda'}\|Q_j\|_{L^4}\le c\,2^{-\Lambda/2}$, while $\|Q_{\le\Lambda}\|_{L^4}\le\|Q\|_{L^4}+c$ keeps the second bounded uniformly in $\Lambda$ and $\Lambda'$. The right side of~\eqref{eq:wick-cauchy} is therefore $O(2^{-\Lambda/2})$ uniformly in $\Lambda'>\Lambda$. Thus,
\begin{equation*}
    \big\|V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda'};D_{\Lambda'})
    -V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)\big\|_{L^2(d\upphi_Q)}
    \;=\;O\big(2^{-\Lambda/4}\big)\;,
\end{equation*}
so the family is Cauchy in $L^2(d\upphi_Q)$ and therefore converges to a limit $V$, which is~\eqref{eq:wick-limit}.

The same computation identifies this limit with the one of \cite[Prop.~8.5.1]{GlimmJaffe1987}, which mollifies the field.  Fix an even $\varrho\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^2)$, nonnegative with integral one, and set $\varrho_\varepsilon(z):=\varepsilon^{-2}\varrho(z/\varepsilon)$.  The mollified field $\upphi_\varepsilon(x):=\langle\upphi,\varrho_\varepsilon(x-\cdot)\rangle$ is a function of $\upphi$ with covariance $Q_\varepsilon:=\varrho_\varepsilon*Q*\varrho_\varepsilon$, and the corresponding interaction is $V_\varepsilon:=t\int g\,H_4\big(\upphi_\varepsilon;Q_\varepsilon(0)\big)\,dx$.  Because the shells are independent, the truncation and the mollified field have cross-covariance $\operatorname{Cov}\big(\upphi_{\le\Lambda}(x),\upphi_\varepsilon(y)\big)=(Q_{\le\Lambda}*\varrho_\varepsilon)(x-y)$, and Hermite orthogonality gives, as in~\eqref{eq:wick-cauchy},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:molli}
    \mathbb{E}_{\upphi_Q}\Big[\big(V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)-V_\varepsilon\big)^2\Big]
    \;=\;4!\,t^2\!\iint g(x)g(y)\Big[Q_{\le\Lambda}^4-2\,(Q_{\le\Lambda}*\varrho_\varepsilon)^4+Q_\varepsilon^4\Big](x-y)\,dx\,dy\;.
\end{equation}
Convolution with $\varrho_\varepsilon$ is a contraction on $L^4(\mathbb{R}^2)$ that converges to the identity as $\varepsilon\to0$, so together with the tail bound above, all three kernels converge to $Q$ in $L^4(\mathbb{R}^2)$ as $\Lambda\to\infty$ and $\varepsilon\to0$.  Write the bracket as $\big[Q_{\le\Lambda}^4-(Q_{\le\Lambda}*\varrho_\varepsilon)^4\big]+\big[Q_\varepsilon^4-(Q_{\le\Lambda}*\varrho_\varepsilon)^4\big]$.  As in~\eqref{eq:fourth-power}, the $L^1$ norm of each term is at most a constant times the $L^4$ distance between its two kernels, and because these distances tend to zero, the right side of~\eqref{eq:molli} tends to zero. Thus $V$ is also the $L^2$ limit of the $V_\varepsilon$, which is $t$ times the smeared Wick power of \cite[Prop.~8.5.1]{GlimmJaffe1987}.

Each $V_\varepsilon$ is a function of $\upphi$, and therefore so is their $L^2$ limit.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Part (b).}  Fix $f_1,\dots,f_r\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^2)$ and a bounded continuous $F:\mathbb{R}^r\to\mathbb{R}$.  By~\eqref{eq:mu-std-def} the measure $P^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$ has density $e^{-V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)}/Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$ against the law of $\upphi_{\le\Lambda}$, so the Gibbs ratio may be read on the space carrying $\upphi$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:LP-ratio}
    \mathbb{E}_{P^{(\infty)}_\Lambda}\big[F\big]
    \;=\;\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\upphi_Q}\big[F\big(\langle\upphi_{\le\Lambda},f_k\rangle\big)\,
    e^{-V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)}\big]}
    {\mathbb{E}_{\upphi_Q}\big[e^{-V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)}\big]}\;.
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:phi42-standard} presents $\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[F]$ as the same ratio with $\upphi$ in place of $\upphi_{\le\Lambda}$ and $V$ in place of $V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)$.  With
\begin{equation*}
    \xi_\Lambda\;:=\;F\big(\langle\upphi_{\le\Lambda},f_k\rangle\big)\,
    e^{-V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)}\;,
    \qquad
    \xi\;:=\;F\big(\langle\upphi,f_k\rangle\big)\,e^{-V}\;,
\end{equation*}
it suffices to show that $\xi_\Lambda\to\xi$ in $L^1(d\upphi_Q)$, and likewise at $F\equiv1$ for the denominator, since $|\mathbb{E}_{\upphi_Q}[\xi_\Lambda]-\mathbb{E}_{\upphi_Q}[\xi]|\le\|\xi_\Lambda-\xi\|_{L^1(d\upphi_Q)}$.  By~\cite[Thm.~5.12]{Kallenberg2021} at $p=1$, convergence in $L^1$ follows from convergence in probability together with uniform integrability of the $|\xi_\Lambda|$.

Convergence in probability holds factor by factor.  The difference $\langle\upphi-\upphi_{\le\Lambda},f_k\rangle$ is centered, with second moment
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}_{\upphi_Q}\big|\langle\upphi-\upphi_{\le\Lambda},f_k\rangle\big|^2
    \;=\;\int\frac{d^2p}{(2\pi)^2}
    \Big(\sum_{j>\Lambda}S_j(p)\Big)\big|\hat f_k(p)\big|^2
    \;\le\;\int_{|p|\ge2^{\Lambda}}\frac{d^2p}{(2\pi)^2}\,
    \frac{\big|\hat f_k(p)\big|^2}{p^2+m^2}\;,
\end{equation}
because the shells above $\Lambda$ are supported in $|p|\ge2^{\Lambda}$.  The right side is the tail of a convergent integral and tends to zero as $\Lambda\to\infty$.  By Chebyshev's inequality, $\langle\upphi_{\le\Lambda},f_k\rangle\to\langle\upphi,f_k\rangle$ in probability for each $k$, and since $F$ is continuous, $F(\langle\upphi_{\le\Lambda},f_k\rangle)\to F(\langle\upphi,f_k\rangle)$ in probability.  Part~(a) gives $V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)\to V$ in $L^2(d\upphi_Q)$, hence in probability, so $e^{-V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)}\to e^{-V}$ in probability, and the product $\xi_\Lambda$ converges to $\xi$ in probability.

A family bounded in $L^2$ is uniformly integrable, so it suffices to bound the second moments of $\xi_\Lambda$ and of its $F\equiv1$ counterpart uniformly in $\Lambda$.

Since $|\xi_\Lambda|\le\|F\|_\infty\,e^{-V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)}$ and squaring $e^{-V}$ doubles the coupling,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:LP-UI}
    \mathbb{E}_{\upphi_Q}|\xi_\Lambda|^2
    \;\le\;\|F\|_\infty^2\,\mathbb{E}_{\upphi_Q}\big[e^{-2V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)}\big]
    \;=\;\|F\|_\infty^2\,Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda(2t)\;.
\end{equation}
It is therefore enough to bound $Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda(2t)$ uniformly in $\Lambda$.  Since $Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda(2t)$ does not involve the features, it suffices to bound it along one realization of $\omega^\infty$ in the probability-one events of \Cref{lem:unif-gg} and \Cref{thm:infwidth}. At each width, \Cref{thm:d2-convergence} at coupling $2t$ and the base bound $Z^{(n)}_0(2t)\le e^{2c_V}$ from \Cref{lem:V-lower} and~\eqref{eq:d2-C1} give
\begin{equation*}
    \log Z^{(n)}_\Lambda(2t)
    \;\le\;\log Z^{(n)}_0(2t)+C_T(2t)\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-2j}j^3
    \;\le\;2c_V+C_T(2t)\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-2j}j^3\;,
\end{equation*}
where $C_T(2t)$ is the constant of \Cref{prop:d2-exp-int} at coupling $2t$, free of $n$ by \Cref{prop:uniform-N}.  The right side depends on neither $\Lambda$ nor $n$.  Because $Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda(2t)$ is the width limit of $Z^{(n)}_\Lambda(2t)$ (\Cref{thm:infwidth}), the same bound holds for $\log Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda(2t)$.  Both families are therefore uniformly integrable.  Together with the convergence in probability, we have $\xi_\Lambda\to\xi$ in $L^1(d\upphi_Q)$, and likewise for the denominators.

The denominators converge to $\int e^{-V}d\upphi_Q$.  Because each $V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)$ is Wick ordered at the variance of $\upphi_{\le\Lambda}$ and has mean zero, its $L^2$ limit $V$ has mean zero too, and Jensen's inequality gives $\int e^{-V}d\upphi_Q\ge 1$.  Hence $\mathbb{E}_{P^{(\infty)}_\Lambda}[F]\to\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[F]$, which is the stated weak convergence on $\mathbb{R}^r$.

Applied to the real and imaginary parts of $u\mapsto\exp(i\sum_ks_ku_k)$, this weak convergence shows that $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\Lambda$ converges pointwise to the characteristic function of $\big(\langle\upphi,f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $\nu$, and~\eqref{eq:interchange} identifies that limit as $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty$.  The second weak limit is then the one given by \Cref{thm:interchange} for the other order of limits.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:d2-construction}
The measure $\nu$ of~\eqref{eq:phi42-standard} is a probability measure on $\mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^2)$.  It is absolutely continuous with respect to the free-field measure $\upphi_Q$, with density $e^{-V}/\int e^{-V}d\upphi_Q$ and normalization $\int e^{-V}d\upphi_Q\in[1,\infty)$.  Almost surely in $\omega^\infty$, for every $r$, all $f_1,\dots,f_r\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^2)$, and either order of the limits $\Lambda\to\infty$ and $n\to\infty$, the law of the smeared vector $\big(\langle\phi_\Lambda^{(n)},f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under the neural-network measure $\mu^{(n)}_\Lambda$ of \Cref{def:nested} converges weakly on $\mathbb{R}^r$ to the law of $\big(\langle\upphi,f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $\nu$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
The proof of \Cref{thm:LP-standard}(b) gives $e^{-V(\upphi_{\le\Lambda};D_\Lambda)}\to e^{-V}$ in $L^1(d\upphi_Q)$, and shows that
\begin{equation*}
    1\;\le\;\int e^{-V}\,d\upphi_Q\;=\;\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}Z^{(\infty)}_\Lambda\;<\;\infty\;.
\end{equation*}
By \Cref{thm:LP-standard}(a), $V$ is a function of the field $\upphi$, so $e^{-V}/\int e^{-V}d\upphi_Q$ is a nonnegative measurable function on $\mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^2)$ with integral one against $\upphi_Q$, and~\eqref{eq:phi42-standard} defines a probability measure absolutely continuous with respect to $\upphi_Q$.

For the convergence, \Cref{thm:interchange} gives weak convergence along either order of limits to the law with characteristic function $\mathcal{X}^{(\infty)}_\infty$, and \Cref{thm:LP-standard}(b) identifies that law as the law of $\big(\langle\upphi,f_k\rangle\big)_{k\le r}$ under $\nu$.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:gff}
\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item There is a unique probability measure $\upphi_Q$ on $\mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:minlos}
    \int_{\mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)}e^{i\langle\upphi,f\rangle}\,d\upphi_Q
    \;=\; e^{-\|f\|_Q^2/2}
    \qquad\text{for every }f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)\;,
\end{equation}
namely the Gaussian free field of covariance $Q=(-\Delta+m^2)^{-1}$.
\item The shell fields can be realized on one probability space as independent centered Gaussian fields $\upphi^{(j)}$ with covariances $Q_j$, together with a field $\upphi$ of law $\upphi_Q$, such that for every $f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ the series $\sum_{j\ge0}\langle\upphi^{(j)},f\rangle$ converges to $\langle\upphi,f\rangle$ almost surely and in $L^2$.  The truncations $\upphi_{\le\Lambda}=\sum_{j\le\Lambda}\upphi^{(j)}$ have covariance $Q_{\le\Lambda}$.  This is the decomposition~\eqref{eq:phi-shells}.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
\textit{Part (a).}  By~\eqref{eq:fQ-norm}, $f\mapsto\|f\|_Q^2$ is a positive semidefinite quadratic form on $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, so $f\mapsto e^{-\|f\|_Q^2/2}$ is positive definite and equals $1$ at $f=0$.  The Bochner--Minlos theorem \cite[Thm.~3.4.2]{GlimmJaffe1987} also asks that this functional be continuous on $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$.  Because $\|\cdot\|_Q$ is a norm and $(p^2+m^2)^{-1}\le m^{-2}$, for $f,f'\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$
\begin{equation*}
    \big|\,\|f\|_Q-\|f'\|_Q\,\big|\;\le\;\|f-f'\|_Q
    \;\le\;m^{-1}\,\|f-f'\|_{L^2}\;,
\end{equation*}
and $\|\cdot\|_{L^2}$ is continuous on $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$.  The Bochner--Minlos theorem therefore gives a unique probability measure $\upphi_Q$ satisfying~\eqref{eq:minlos}, and by~\eqref{eq:fQ-norm} it is the centered Gaussian measure of covariance $Q$.

\medskip\noindent\textit{Part (b).}  Part~(a) with $S_j$ in place of $(p^2+m^2)^{-1}$ gives, for each $j$, a centered Gaussian measure on $\mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ with covariance $Q_j$.  Take the $\upphi^{(j)}$ independent with these laws.  A finite sum of independent centered Gaussian fields is centered Gaussian with the sum of the covariances, which for $\upphi_{\le\Lambda}$ is $Q_{\le\Lambda}$.

To construct $\upphi$, fix $s>d/2$, set $w(x):=(1+|x|^2)^{-d}$, and let $H$ be the Hilbert space of distributions $u$ with $\|u\|_H:=\|(1-\Delta)^{-s/2}(wu)\|_{L^2}<\infty$, which embeds continuously in $\mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$.  Since the $S_j$ sum to $(p^2+m^2)^{-1}\le m^{-2}$ by~\eqref{eq:dyadic}, the covariance operators satisfy $\sum_jQ_j=Q\le m^{-2}$ on $L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$, and therefore
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{j\ge0}\mathbb{E}\big\|\upphi^{(j)}\big\|_H^2
    \;\le\;m^{-2}\,\|w\|_{L^2}^2\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\frac{d^dp}{(2\pi)^d}\,(1+p^2)^{-s}
    \;<\;\infty\;.
\end{equation*}
Because the shells are independent and centered, for $\Lambda<\Lambda'$
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\big\|\upphi_{\le\Lambda'}-\upphi_{\le\Lambda}\big\|_H^2
    \;=\;\sum_{\Lambda<j\le\Lambda'}\mathbb{E}\big\|\upphi^{(j)}\big\|_H^2\;,
\end{equation*}
a tail of the convergent series above.  The truncations are therefore Cauchy in mean square in $H$, and they converge in mean square to an $H$-valued random field $\upphi$.  Pairing with $f$ is continuous on $H$, so $\sum_{j\le\Lambda}\langle\upphi^{(j)},f\rangle\to\langle\upphi,f\rangle$ in $L^2$.  The summands are independent and centered, with variances $2\theta_j$ summing to $\|f\|_Q^2$ by~\eqref{eq:theta-j}, so the series also converges almost surely, and $\langle\upphi,f\rangle$ is centered Gaussian with variance $\|f\|_Q^2$.  By the uniqueness in part~(a), the law of $\upphi$ is $\upphi_Q$.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:shell-char}
For $f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, write $I(b,c):=\int f(x)\cos(b\cdot x+c)\,d^dx$, and let $\mathbb{E}_{b,c}$ average over $(b,c)\sim\pi_j\otimes\mathrm{Unif}([-\pi,\pi])$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shell-char}
    \mathbb{E}\big[e^{i\langle\varphi_j,f\rangle}\big]
    \;=\;\Big(\mathbb{E}_{b,c}\big[
    e^{-q_jI(b,c)^2/N^{(n)}_j}\big]\Big)^{N^{(n)}_j}
    \;>\;0\;,
\end{equation}
and its logarithm differs from $-\theta_j$, the logarithm of the free-field value, by at most a constant times $1/N^{(n)}_j$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shell-rate}
    0 \;\le\; \log\mathbb{E}\big[e^{i\langle\varphi_j,f\rangle}\big]+\theta_j
    \;\le\; \frac{\|f\|_{L^1}^2\,\theta_j}{2}\cdot
    \frac{q_j}{N^{(n)}_j}\;.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Write $N:=N^{(n)}_j$ and $X:=q_j\,I(b,c)^2\ge0$, so that the $N$ features of shell $j$ give independent copies $X_1,\dots,X_N$ of $X$.  Conditionally on the features, the smeared shell field
\begin{equation*}
    \langle\varphi_j,f\rangle
    \;=\;\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}a_{j,\ell}\,
    \sqrt{\frac{2q_j}{N\sigma^2}}\;I(b_{j,\ell},c_{j,\ell})
\end{equation*}
is a centered Gaussian of variance $\tfrac{2}{N}\sum_\ell X_\ell$, so its conditional characteristic function is $\prod_\ell e^{-X_\ell/N}$.  Averaging over the independent features gives $\mathbb{E}[e^{i\langle\varphi_j,f\rangle}]=\big(\mathbb{E}_{b,c}[e^{-X/N}]\big)^N$, which is~\eqref{eq:shell-char}.

Since $I(b,c)=\tfrac12\big(e^{ic}\hat f(-b)+e^{-ic}\hat f(b)\big)$, $\mathbb{E}_c[e^{\pm2ic}]=0$, and $\hat f(-b)=\overline{\hat f(b)}$ for real $f$, only the cross term of $I(b,c)^2$ survives the average over $c$, and $\mathbb{E}_c[I(b,c)^2]=\tfrac12|\hat f(b)|^2$.  Averaging over $b\sim\pi_j$ and multiplying by $q_j$ gives $\mathbb{E}_{b,c}[X]=\theta_j$, so the quantity in~\eqref{eq:shell-rate} is $N\log\mathbb{E}_{b,c}[e^{-X/N}]+\mathbb{E}_{b,c}[X]$.  Jensen's inequality for $u\mapsto e^{-u}$ shows that it is nonnegative.  For the upper bound, $e^{-u}\le1-u+\tfrac12u^2$ on $u\ge0$ gives
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}_{b,c}\big[e^{-X/N}\big]
    \;\le\; 1-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{b,c}[X]}{N}+\frac{\mathbb{E}_{b,c}[X^2]}{2N^2}\;,
\end{equation*}
and the right side is positive because it dominates the left.  Taking logarithms with $\log(1+w)\le w$ and multiplying by $N$ bounds the quantity by $\mathbb{E}_{b,c}[X^2]/(2N)$.  Finally, $|I(b,c)|\le\|f\|_{L^1}$ gives $X\le q_j\|f\|_{L^1}^2$, hence $\mathbb{E}_{b,c}[X^2]\le q_j\|f\|_{L^1}^2\,\theta_j$, which is~\eqref{eq:shell-rate}.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:free-field}
For every $f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, both iterated limits of $\upchi^{(n)}_\Lambda(f)$ exist and agree,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:char-limit}
    \lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}
    \upchi^{(n)}_\Lambda(f)
    \;=\;\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}
    \upchi^{(n)}_\Lambda(f)
    \;=\; e^{-\|f\|_Q^2/2}\;,
\end{equation}
which by~\eqref{eq:minlos} is the characteristic functional of the free field $\upphi_Q$.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
By~\eqref{eq:shell-char}, every factor in~\eqref{eq:char-factorize} is strictly positive, so $\upchi^{(n)}_\Lambda(f)>0$, and~\eqref{eq:char-factorize} gives
\begin{equation*}
    r_{n,\Lambda}\;:=\;\log\upchi^{(n)}_\Lambda(f)
    +\sum_{j\le\Lambda}\theta_j
    \;=\;\sum_{j\le\Lambda}\Big(
    \log\mathbb{E}\big[e^{i\langle\varphi_j,f\rangle}\big]+\theta_j\Big)\;.
\end{equation*}
By~\eqref{eq:shell-rate} each summand is nonnegative, so $r_{n,\Lambda}$ is nondecreasing in $\Lambda$, and summing the upper bounds with $\sum_j\theta_j=\tfrac12\|f\|_Q^2$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ann-rate}
    0 \;\le\; r_{n,\Lambda}
    \;\le\; \frac{\|f\|_{L^1}^2\,\|f\|_Q^2}{4}\,
    \sup_{j\ge0}\frac{q_j}{N^{(n)}_j}\;.
\end{equation}
By \Cref{lem:shell-dim}(a), $q_j\le C\,2^{(d-2)j}$, and the schedule~\eqref{eq:width-schedule} gives $N^{(n)}_j\ge nc_0(j{+}1)2^{2dj}$, so
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{q_j}{N^{(n)}_j}
    \;\le\;\frac{C}{n\,c_0}\,2^{-(d+2)j}
    \;\le\;\frac{C}{n\,c_0}\;.
\end{equation*}
Hence $0\le r_{n,\Lambda}\le C'/n$ with $C'$ free of $\Lambda$.  Taking $n\to\infty$ first, $r_{n,\Lambda}\to0$ at each fixed $\Lambda$, so the inner limit of $\log\upchi^{(n)}_\Lambda(f)$ is $-\sum_{j\le\Lambda}\theta_j$, and the outer one is $-\tfrac12\|f\|_Q^2$.  Taking $\Lambda\to\infty$ first, $r_{n,\Lambda}$ is nondecreasing and bounded, so it converges at each fixed $n$ to some $r_n\in[0,C'/n]$.  The inner limit is then $-\tfrac12\|f\|_Q^2+r_n$, and letting $n\to\infty$ gives $-\tfrac12\|f\|_Q^2$.  Exponentiating gives~\eqref{eq:char-limit}.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
Under $\gamma_{J-1}$, for fixed $x,y\in K$ the background $B_{J-1}(x)$ and the increment $B_{J-1}(x)-B_{J-1}(y)$ are centered Gaussians with variances
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:d2-sup-var}
\begin{align}
    \operatorname{Var}\big(B_{J-1}(x)\big)
    &\;=\;D^\omega_{J-1}(x)\;\le\; c_qJ\;,
    \label{eq:d2-sup-var-a}\\
    \operatorname{Var}\big(B_{J-1}(x)-B_{J-1}(y)\big)
    &\;\le\; \Upsilon_{J-1}\,|x-y|^2\;.
    \label{eq:d2-sup-var-b}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
The first is~\eqref{eq:sigmaJ} together with $D^\omega_{J-1}\le c_qJ$, and the second is \Cref{lem:incr-var} at index $J-1$.

For $k\ge J$ let $\Delta_k\subset K$ be a $2^{-k}$-net over $K$ with $|\Delta_k|\le c_K4^k$. We emphasize that the index $k$ labels spatial resolution, not a shell. Fix $x\in K$ and let $x_k\in\Delta_k$ be a nearest net point, so that $|x-x_k|\le2^{-k}$ and $|x_k-x_{k-1}|\le3\cdot2^{-k}$.  Since $B_{J-1}$ is a finite sum of cosines, it is continuous, so $B_{J-1}(x_k)\to B_{J-1}(x)$ and
\begin{equation*}
    B_{J-1}(x) \;=\; B_{J-1}(x_J)
    +\sum_{k>J}\Big[B_{J-1}(x_k)
    -B_{J-1}(x_{k-1})\Big]\;.
\end{equation*}
Split the threshold $s$ into $s/2$ for $B_{J-1}(x_J)$ and $u_k:=\tfrac{s}{8}\,2^{-(k-J)/2}$ for the $k$-th increment, so that $\sum_{k>J}u_k\le s/2$.  If $|B_{J-1}(x)|>s$, then either $\max_{\Delta_J}|B_{J-1}|>s/2$, or, for some $k>J$, a pair of points from $\Delta_{k-1}$ and $\Delta_k$ at distance at most $3\cdot2^{-k}$ has increment exceeding $u_k$ in absolute value. Neither event depends on $x$, so one of them occurs whenever $\sup_K|B_{J-1}|>s$.

By~\eqref{eq:d2-sup-var-a} and the Gaussian tail $\gamma(|Z|>u)\le2e^{-u^2/(2v)}$ for a centered Gaussian $Z$ with variance~$v$,
\begin{equation*}
    \gamma_{J-1}\Big(\{\max_{y\,\in\Delta_J}|B_{J-1}(y)|>s/2\}\Big)\leq2c_K4^Je^{-s^2/(8c_qJ)}\;.
\end{equation*}
At $k=J+m$ ($m\ge1$), there are at most $|\Delta_k|\cdot|\Delta_{k-1}|\leq c_K^2\,4^{2k}$ pairs, each with increment variance at most $9\Upsilon_{J-1}4^{-k}$ by~\eqref{eq:d2-sup-var-b}.  Then the probability that one of these pairs has increment exceeding $u_k$ in absolute value is at most
\begin{equation*}
    2c_K^2\,16^{J}\,16^{m}\,e^{-\xi\,2^{m}},
    \qquad
    \xi:=\frac{s^2}{3072\,q_{\max}}\;,
\end{equation*}
using $u_k^2\,4^{k}=\tfrac{s^2}{64}\,4^{J}2^{m}$ and $18\Upsilon_{J-1}\le48\,q_{\max}4^J$.  Set
\begin{equation*}
    c_2:=\frac{1}{3072}\;,
    \qquad
    C_K:=\max\{16,\,2c_K+4c_K^2\}\;,
\end{equation*}
and distinguish two cases according to the size of $\xi$.

If $\xi\ge2\ln16$, then $m\ln16\le\tfrac{\xi}{2}2^m$ gives $16^me^{-\xi2^m}\le e^{-\xi2^{m-1}}\le e^{-\xi m}$, and the levels sum to at most $\sum_{m\geq1}e^{-\xi m}\leq2e^{-\xi}$.  Collecting, and using $q_{\max}\le c_qJ$ in the second exponent,
\begin{equation*}
    \gamma_{J-1}\Big(\sup_K|B_{J-1}|>s\Big)
    \;\le\; 2c_K\,4^Je^{-s^2/(8c_qJ)}
    +4c_K^2\,16^{J}e^{-s^2/(3072\,q_{\max})}
    \;\le\; C_K\,16^J
    \exp\!\Big(-\frac{c_2\,s^2}{c_qJ}\Big)\;.
\end{equation*}
If instead $\xi<2\ln16$, then $c_2s^2/(c_qJ)\le\xi<2\ln16$. The right side of \eqref{eq:d2-sup-tail} then already exceeds $1$, so the bound holds trivially.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
